Abstract: Social robots face diverse and changing tasks in companionship and education. LLM-based methods can generate task programs, but they still rely on manually developed control interfaces and predefined capabilities. They also lack a general mechanism to optimize completed tasks based on user-specified metrics and reuse performance improvements across tasks.We present EvoMisty, a plugin-based framework that enables coding agents to build and continually improve robot capabilities. EvoMisty builds a control program from official documentation and verifies candidate capabilities on a physical robot. Verified capabilities are stored in an atomic capability library and combined with existing skills to solve tasks. After task completion, EvoMisty can collect execution data, measure user-specified metrics, and iteratively improve task programs. It then distills reusable skills from final solutions into a skill library for future tasks.We evaluate EvoMisty on a physical Misty II robot using Claude Code and Codex. The two agents construct 109 and 107 verified atomic capabilities, respectively. Both complete all 28 AutoMisty benchmark tasks and seven additional tasks beyond AutoMisty's capabilities. Optimization improves all seven evaluated metrics across five tasks. For example, dialogue response latency decreases by 52% and 71%, respectively. Reusing skills from one optimized dialogue task reduces latency by 33% to 72% in three other dialogue tasks without further optimization. All code and experimental videos will be publicly released through the webpage: https://wangxiaoshawn.github.io/projects/evomisty/index.html

</details>

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: When people intervene to correct a robot, they provide information not only through how they correct its behavior, but also through when they choose to intervene. Existing approaches to Learning from Corrections focus on comparing the new trajectory to the robot's originally intended behavior, thus underutilizing the context of the robot's behavior before the intervention. In this work, we investigate how both pre- and post-intervention behavior can be translated into preference pairs for learning value functions that approximate a human's latent reward. We construct post-intervention preferences by comparing corrected behavior with the behavior it replaces, and pre-intervention preferences from temporal relationships among states leading up to an intervention. We evaluate the learned value representations through two downstream tasks: goal inference and correction forecasting. Our results show that post-intervention preferences are particularly effective for goal inference, while pre-intervention preferences provide strong correction-forecasting performance. Combining both sources of information performs strongly across both tasks, even when generalizing to participants unseen during training. These findings demonstrate that both when and how humans correct provide complementary information for learning value representations from corrective interactions.

</details>

Abstract: This paper presents a rigorous geometric accessibility and controllability analysis for a fixed-wing Uncrewed Aircraft System (UAS) actuated solely by an internal moving mass, a momentum wheel, and external thrust. This configuration eliminates traditional control surfaces, which in turn reduces the combinatorial set of computational fluid dynamics (CFD) studies necessary for high-fidelity modeling of flight through unsteady flow fields. Using Lie bracket computations and the strong Lie Algebra Rank Condition, it is shown that the full nonlinear dynamics are generically small-time locally accessible, with singular configurations explicitly characterized and shown to be immediately escapable by direct mass actuation. The stronger property of small-time local controllability is shown to hold for the attitude subsystem at equilibrium points, using Sussmann's condition. A key contribution is the explicit treatment of the time-varying inertia tensor via the Huygens-Steiner Theorem, which introduces nontrivial coupling between the control vector fields. The momentum wheel provides direct yaw authority that, combined with inertia variation from the moving mass, generates a full-rank accessibility Lie algebra. A nonlinear Model Predictive Controller (MPC) is derived with consideration of flight envelope constraints and is applied to track different trajectories under unperturbed and perturbed flow field conditions.

</details>

Abstract: This paper introduces CRAB H as a task-centric certification methodology for healthcare autonomous systems. CRAB H extends the ARC governance architecture by introducing the task as a first-class governance object, represented through Task Requirement Profiles TRP H. Each profile defines the cognitive capabilities, governance constraints, evidentiary requirements, and operational assumptions necessary for the safe execution of a specific healthcare activity. Rather than asking whether a robot achieves a sufficiently high benchmark score, CRAB H asks whether demonstrated capabilities satisfy the requirements of the task. Certification becomes a comparison between demonstrated capability and required capability. A reliability-gated assurance allocation mechanism routes cognitive dimensions that cannot be reproducibly evaluated to LEA operational requirements rather than discarding them. Governance integrity is preserved: unreliable dimensions are governed, not ignored. The Capability Margin is defined as the threshold-based conjunction of per-dimension compliance determinations, not as a subtracted scalar preserving non compensatory governance logic appropriate for safety-critical healthcare deployment

</details>

Abstract: Current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) end-to-end systems have driven major progress in robot learning, yet they still face persistent barriers to dependable real-world deployment. In practice, these models often exhibit brittle generalization under distribution shift (i.e., novel objects, layouts, lighting, or contact conditions), limited physical and causal reasoning, and inconsistent fine-grained motor control. Critically, many VLA pipelines offer limited ways to express and audit what a robot must not do, making it difficult to provide assurance in safety-critical settings. These limitations constrain scalability, maintainability, and trust when robots move beyond controlled demonstrations. We propose Embodied Neuro-Symbolic (NeSy) AI: a deployment-oriented paradigm that integrates learned perception, planning and control with explicit, machine-readable background knowledge and formal constraints. By combining knowledge-guided perception, constraint-aware symbolic planning, and hybrid learning, Embodied NeSy AI enables robots to reason about their environment, adapt to new tasks, and provide auditable, machine-checkable compliance with safety and operational requirements, thus supporting scalable, trustworthy robot deployment.

</details>

Abstract: While recent vision-language-action (VLA) models increasingly incorporate 3D geometry, how to effectively exploit it for precise and generalizable action prediction remains a central challenge. We introduce Hi3R-VLA, a 3D policy that grounds heterogeneous modalities - vision, language, and action - in explicit 3D geometry for robotic manipulation. Our model integrates spatially-aligned visual features and language conditioning into point cloud representations, enriching scene geometry with fine-grained, task-relevant semantics. It then anchors low-level control commands in the same 3D coordinate frame and uses 3D-RoPE enhanced action-to-point attention over coarse-to-fine point features to model geometric relationships between action targets and observed points. Our analysis demonstrates the importance of semantic fusion within 3D and spatial alignment between observations and actions. With fewer than 250M trainable parameters, Hi3R-VLA achieves state-of-the-art performance on RoboCasa, RLBench, RoboTwin2, DexJoCo and DexSuite across diverse manipulation tasks and embodiments, from single-arm and dual-arm grippers to dexterous hands. Real-robot evaluations further demonstrate its effectiveness in the physical world. The model, code and dataset are publicly released at https://cshizhe.github.io/projects/hi3r_vla.html.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-agent trajectory optimization poses two coupled challenges: effectively exploring non-convex landscapes and scaling favorably to large teams of agents. Sampling-based methods are natural candidates for the exploration challenge but scale poorly with problem dimension. Moreover, their off-the-shelf integration into distributed optimization introduces a fundamental trade-off: sampling either fails to address the constraint-induced non-convexity or its search space grows with neighborhood size. We address this gap by introducing a $\underline{D}\text{istributed}$ $\underline{S}\text{ampling-based}$ $\underline{T}\text{rajectory}$ $\underline{O}\text{ptimization} \ \text{(DS-TO)}$ architecture built on a splitting mechanism tailored to sampling. Under this splitting, each agent samples only in its own control space, independent of neighborhood size, while still remaining exposed to both the constraint-induced non-convexity and the decisions of other agents. The resulting algorithm is fully decentralized, requiring only neighborhood communication, with all local updates executed in parallel across agents. Efficient variations, including parallel Jacobi updates within each agent's subproblem and single-step constraint linearization, further reduce computation. Experiments on 2D and 3D tasks with up to 1024 agents demonstrate improved exploration and constraint satisfaction over state-of-the-art derivative-based distributed optimization, and superior scalability to existing distributed sampling methods.

</details>

Abstract: Fabric manipulation challenges robot policies with changing geometry, self-occlusion, and contact, yet standardized evaluation remains focused on rigid objects. We introduce FabricBench, a benchmark for bimanual fabric manipulation with six tasks spanning garments, silk, and bags, 10,000 simulation-ready assets, and controlled tests of configuration and appearance generalization. Built on an open-source solver in Newton, our simulator enables stable bimanual grasping, holding, and release through contact forces, without artificial attachment. Redesigned cloth-rigid contact and bidirectional force coupling support interactions among robot arms, fabric, and free rigid objects, allowing a bag to support its contents and transmit their weight to the grippers. In a controlled grasp-hold-release evaluation, our solver succeeds in all 45 trials, compared with none for the tested baseline configurations. Evaluating six representative policies, including vision-language-action models and world action models, reveals substantial remaining challenges: the best policy achieves 77% average success, while no policy exceeds 36% on long-horizon T-shirt folding. Under the most deformed initial T-shirt configurations, success falls to 6-14% across policies, with larger drops than under the evaluated appearance shifts. Zero-shot deployment of $π_{0.5}$ on a real robot largely preserves task difficulty ordering across six tasks (Spearman $ρ=0.94$). These results establish FabricBench as a reproducible testbed for measuring fabric-manipulation capabilities and exposing limitations in configuration generalization. The project website is available at https://fabricbench.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract: In underwater environments, Global Positioning System (GPS) signals and high-speed radio communication are unavailable, and severe constraints exist on communication bandwidth and visibility; consequently, applying conventional swarm formation methods, such as the Boids model, which require the relative positions and velocity vectors of neighboring vehicles, is difficult. This study proposes a decentralized swarm control model that operates without a leader or absolute coordinates, relying solely on the number of received signals from two communication channels with different transmission ranges (inner and outer neighborhoods). The proposed model extends a cellular automaton model based on Turing pattern formation principles to continuous space and is grounded in the fundamental concept that swarms form as robots move toward regions of higher potential calculated from the difference in received signal counts. Specifically, stable behavior is determined through temporal averaging of potential values, hysteresis-based state evaluation, stepwise turning based on temporal differences, and vector composition with an external directional command. Two-dimensional simulation results demonstrated that autonomous swarm formation and control of swarm spatial dispersion via a morphological parameter are achievable, confirming robustness in maintaining swarms and high polarization even under communication dropouts where the communication success rate decreases to approximately 0.7.

</details>

Abstract: Although radiance fields provide detailed scene representations for robotics, they remain underexplored for trajectory planning, without a widely agreed-upon way to translate learned spatial primitives into reliable collision criteria. To bridge this gap, we introduce Collision Rendering, the first trajectory planning framework on sparse voxel radiance fields (SVR), which supports GPU-accelerated, risk-bounded collision checking and a differentiable collision cost. Our key insight is that the structured volumetric density of SVR allows its rendering pipeline to serve as a fast numerical approximator of collision probability. We approximate the robot with collision spheres and place a coinciding spherical camera at each sphere. Under a probabilistic collision model, we derive an upper bound on each sphere's maximum directional collision probability from the camera's rendering pipeline. Evaluating this bound on the GPU yields a modular collision checker that integrates with established sampling-based planners without an auxiliary geometric map or changes to the learned representation. We further compute a collision cost and its gradients from rendered opacities through the same pipeline, extending the framework to trajectory optimization. Experiments demonstrate millisecond collision checking time on a consumer-grade GPU and collision-free trajectories in all queries, outperforming other radiance field-based planners.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles rely on cameras, LiDAR, and radar to perceive their surroundings, yet these exteroceptive sensors can be degraded by hardware aging, environmental interference, miscalibration, embedded software errors, and combinations of these causes, with direct consequences for perception quality and safety. Existing surveys and benchmarks mostly address individual sensors, isolated conditions, or vehicle-level consequences, and no common vocabulary relates fault origins across the three modalities. This paper reviews the literature on exteroceptive sensor faults and proposes a cross-modal, origin-driven taxonomy that organizes them into five categories, namely hardware, environmental, calibration, software, and synergistic faults, mapping each entry to its physical or logical mechanism, observable manifestation, and affected subsystem, and relating the categories to the safety analyses of ISO 26262 and ISO 21448. To make the taxonomy operational, we release an open-source fault injection toolkit with 94 symptom-level routines (41 camera, 27 LiDAR, and 26 radar), four graded severity levels, reproducible burst scheduling, and per-frame labels recording the fault category, affected subsystem, and severity. Examples on the Boreas dataset illustrate how each category alters camera images, LiDAR point clouds, and radar scans. Together, the taxonomy and the toolkit provide a shared reference and a controlled testbed for studying sensor reliability, developing and benchmarking fault detection and diagnosis methods, and building fault-annotated datasets for the safety validation of perception systems. The toolkit is publicly available at https://github.com/davidepossenti/AV-SensorFault-Toolkit.

</details>

Abstract: Differentiable simulation enables gradient-based optimization in learning and control problems involving collisions, but its gradients are least reliable at the moment of impact, when the velocities jump. A fixed-step simulator detects a collision only after the bodies overlap, and some differentiable simulators correct for this with a linear estimate of the time of impact. In this paper, we study impacts between ellipsoids. We show that the gradient error of this estimate grows with the obliqueness of the collision, the curvature at contact, and the time step, and that ignoring spin adds a further error. Subsequently, we find the time of impact with continuous collision detection on the Perram Wertheim contact function and differentiate through the touching condition rather than the search. This gives exact gradients at any step size. We demonstrate our method on an optimal control problem and a system identification problem. In both, exact gradients recover the optimal control and the true parameters, while the linear estimate does not, even when it accounts for spin.

</details>

Abstract: A language-model robot agent must decide whether to execute, question or refuse an instruction, and an unsafe execution costs far more than an unneeded question. No prior robot gate derives all its thresholds from error costs or acts on calibrated probabilities, and none was fitted in one domain and evaluated unchanged in another. We propose CATWAY, a cost-sensitive three-way decision gate: a loss matrix over four instruction conditions (clear, ambiguous, infeasible, unsafe) fixes execute, ask and refuse regions on the probability simplex, the question being the information-gathering step. Its probabilities fuse a ten-sample vote with graded scores of ambiguity, infeasibility and risk by logistic regression. Fitted on household instructions and frozen, CATWAY was tested on text-level decisions of three small models in-domain, in two new robot domains and on the external TRUST-NAV benchmark. At eleven generations per decision against one, it was significantly cheaper than a direct prompt, a cost-aware prompt and an unguarded agent in all 18 household comparisons (mean loss per item 0.28 against 0.57-0.83), in 9 of 18 in the new domains and in 10 of 18 on TRUST-NAV (Holm per set), and never significantly costlier. It executed none of the 142 unsafe test instructions. On a unanimous vote, the minimum-expected-loss rule reduces calibration to one loss-derived threshold, so for two of six model configurations a gate on the vote alone never executed a clear instruction, costing 0.49 per item on TRUST-NAV against CATWAY's 0.13 and 0.09; the fused estimate avoided this on every set.

</details>

Abstract: To manipulate a moving object, a policy must predict the scene at the moment its action executes, yet every millisecond spent predicting pushes that moment further away. Scene-level forecasts are computationally expensive, whereas target-centric forecasts can omit nearby objects that affect contact. Our key insight is that what a policy must predict decouples by role: only the target's pose, which determines contact, requires millimeter precision, while the surroundings matter primarily through the clearance they leave and the motion they impart. We present FoveaWAM, a native 4D world-action model that allocates precision according to these roles. Specifically, it models the target's past and future motion as a metric SE(3) trajectory, the surroundings as sparse 3D flow, and relies on the current frame for everything static. This compact representation lets FoveaWAM act in 15.1 ms on one RTX 5090, about 10 times faster than video world-action baselines, leaving substantially less time for the scene to evolve before action execution. To evaluate each policy under its own latency, we introduce OnTimeBench, a dynamic manipulation benchmark in which the scene keeps moving while the policy computes. FoveaWAM reaches 82.1% success on OnTimeBench, 20.8 points above the strongest baseline, and also leads on DOMINO and real-robot tasks. Project page: https://foveawam.github.io/

</details>

Abstract: This paper demonstrates an approach for correcting failures of robot foundation models (RFMs) without additional data collection, demonstrations, or training. We apply weight-space programming (WSP), in which a language-model agent directly reads and writes a neural network's parameters, to a large, pre-trained vision-language-action policy. The idea is simple: many policy failures are not random but repeatedly land in the same recognizable stuck state, so if that state is visible in the model's internal activations, a small edit could trigger a corrective movement while leaving the rest of the model's parameters unchanged. A core design choice is where to write the edit: repurposing a few near-silent neurons in the action expert lets the edit switch on in situations associated with the failure and produce a corrective movement, such as lowering the gripper toward the target, that is specified directly rather than learned through training. Empirically, when applied to the pi0.5 policy in simulation, we find that edits to roughly 0.0002% of the parameters can improve success on recurring failures, including for a policy that was already fine-tuned on its task. Reversed and random edits indicate that the gains come from the intended behavior, and the approach works best when the failure situation is recognizable and a simple correction can address it.

</details>

Abstract: Detect-and-avoid (DAA) systems for autonomous drones operating in shared airspace must select avoidance maneuvers under conditions that differ markedly from those assumed by conventional DAA architectures designed for crewed aircraft. Limited on-board sensing capabilities restrict detection of collision risk to near distances (sub-km), the time available for decision-making is severely limited, uncertainty in the predicted closest point of approach (CPA) and the time to reach it is substantial, and the maneuver envelope of a small uncrewed aircraft at low altitude is tightly constrained.
  This paper presents and compares two maneuver-selection strategies that address these challenges: (1) a purely geometric approach, steering in the direction away from the intruder at CPA, and (2) a method that also takes into account the known sources of uncertainty in the intruder's location, and steers away from a plane expected to contain the intruder's location distribution at CPA.
  Performance is evaluated using the risk ratio metric on a large dataset of simulated near-collision encounters with crewed intruders. Results indicate that both strategies are feasible (i.e., computable in closed form from an onboard track estimate), and that the second strategy substantially outperforms the first.

</details>

Abstract: Bimanual dexterous manipulation relies on multimodal inputs that capture visual context and non-visual physical interaction cues for precise and coordinated control. To address the challenge of effectively integrating these modalities for policy learning, we propose HiMoDex, a two-stage framework centered on hierarchical multimodal world-state representations. In Stage 1, we pretrain a hierarchical representation network on the collected dataset of 1.19 million synchronized multimodal time steps. The network integrates visual context with tactile, force-torque, and audio cues into unified world-state embeddings. In Stage 2, we freeze the pretrained network and use its embeddings to condition a shared world-action transformer. The transformer jointly learns action generation and action-conditioned future-state prediction. Experiments across five real-world tasks show that HiMoDex achieves an average success rate of 78.7\%, representing an absolute improvement of 32.0\% over the strongest evaluated baseline. Extensive ablations validate the benefits of hierarchical representation learning and two-stage training. Failure analysis helps explain performance differences across variants. Code, data, and demos will be released at https://anonymous.4open.science/w/research-website-BAB4/.

Abstract: Released autonomous-driving models achieve strong benchmark performance, yet deploying them on a new real vehicle remains challenging. We study this deployment gap and find that released models can remain coupled to assumptions from their source sensing, data, and execution processes even after conventional adaptation. We introduce Released-to-Real (R2R), a deployment-localization methodology that identifies such source-system coupling and corrects only the affected components while preserving the released model. For data and behavior adaptation, we find that the mismatch between trajectory-level optimization and receding-horizon execution enters through the ego state rather than the observation, and introduce Human-Anchored Reinforcement Learning (HA-RL), which corrects it on recorded target driving without the costly rollouts of generative closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). For physical sensor adaptation, R2R localizes and corrects source-dependent spatial and temporal representations with frozen perception weights, requiring neither target annotations nor retraining. HA-RL improves the closed-loop success rate of a released diffusion-based planner from 82.2% to 95.2%, while sensor adaptation across multiple released detectors and three real sensor rigs improves the vehicle mean average precision (mAP) of a released multi-camera detector from 0.041 to 0.420 on a five-camera target rig. These results provide a practical path for preparing released models before deployment on new real vehicles. Our project page is publicly available at https://released-to-real.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Actor-critic methods achieve high returns in robotic control, yet trained actors exhibit high-frequency action oscillations. Most smoothness methods constrain the actor, whereas PAVE regularizes the critic by separately penalizing action-gradient variation and encouraging negative action curvature. The same gradient change, however, moves the critic's preferred action farther where the critic is flat, and separate penalties do not measure this motion. We characterize this motion through the greedy shift, which approximates greedy-action displacement across state transitions to first order under local concavity. Since displacement alone does not reflect its critic-value consequences, the analysis further shows that the shift's curvature-weighted energy approximates the value lost by retaining the previous greedy action to second order. Based on this relationship, POISE regularizes the curvature-weighted energy along temporal and spatial state displacements. It employs a saddle-free curvature metric and reward-scale-consistent scaling while leaving the actor unchanged. On six continuous control tasks with TD3, POISE reduces the smoothness score on every task by $5.9\times$ on average ($4.5\times$ geometric mean), compared with $2.2\times$ ($1.9\times$) for the strongest baseline, and achieves the lowest score on five tasks. Paired return intervals include zero on four tasks, with a 14% increase on Ant and an 11% decrease on Hopper.

</details>

Abstract: Reactive robotic manipulation is acutely sensitive to simultaneous sensing noise, measurement dropouts, joint execution errors, and contact parameter variations. While single-frame frameworks like Domain-Randomized Instance Set (DRIS) achieve strong robustness under isolated uncertainties (86.4%), they degrade severely when perception and actuation uncertainties co-occur (27.6% across 10 coupled stress scenarios) due to memoryless encoding and fixed high-stiffness PD control. We propose Co-DRIS + SuperMimic, a perception-action co-design framework coupling: (i) an inertial world-frame physics-informed world model combining exact ballistic kinematics with a zero-initialized contact residual to roll forward missing observations and accelerate training via zero-cost counterfactual dropout consistency; (ii) an action-conditioned dual-stream causal GRU belief encoder resolving the denoising-vs-lag deadlock; and (iii) phase-aware variable-impedance soft-catching that transitions the 7-DoF Franka Research 3 arm into an overdamped regime (zeta/zeta0 ~ 2.26x) at the torque layer. Under unbiased 3-seed tail evaluation on unseen throws (15,360 throws/scenario), our method achieves 89.6 +/- 0.4% on standard benchmarks (+10.5% on 4-sigma noise to 70.5%) and 66.6 +/- 0.5% across 10 coupled/dropout stress scenarios (+39.0% over DRIS; +30.4% on 50% dropout). We further validate impedance decoupling, causal contact triggering under +0.65 s flight-time shifts, and cross-task transfer to steel-plate ping-pong bouncing (87.5% to 100.0%) and FR3+Hand catching (+8.4% under 4x noise with 6-13% lower peak impact force).

Abstract: Visual self-models learn from images how a robot moves under motor commands. Continuum robots need them even more, as they bend continuously without measured joint angles or fixed link geometry. Existing continuum self-models predict point clouds, image-space curves, or future images, but not the next 3D shape, appearance, and tip pose together from an observed body. 3D Gaussians offer a renderable representation of shape and appearance, yet articulated Gaussian self-models rely on joint angles to drive deformation. In this work, we propose BendGS, which uses a virtual backbone estimated from observation to drive command-conditioned Gaussian deformation, enabling next-state prediction for continuum robots without measured joint angles. In Gaussian modeling, a feed-forward network reconstructs the robot as 3D Gaussians from multi-view images, and the backbone, a chain of coordinate frames, is fitted to back-projected depth points to estimate the bending state. In transition, a network predicts its change under a relative motor command to deform the bound Gaussians. The deformed Gaussians render the next images and the 3D shape, and the distal frame gives the tip pose. Training losses use RGB-D images and masks, without bending or tip labels. On simulated and real robots, BendGS achieves the lowest Chamfer distance, best image quality, and lowest tip errors among the compared methods.

Abstract: Behavior Foundation Models (BFMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose humanoid control through pretraining on large-scale behavioral data. However, their proficiency in whole-body coordination does not readily extend to scene interaction, which requires adapting motion to scene-dependent geometric constraints and contact dynamics. To bridge this gap, we introduce Ada-BFM, a post-training framework that adapts pretrained BFMs for humanoid scene interaction. Ada-BFM augments the original architecture with a lightweight adapter, enabling interaction-specific adjustments while leveraging pretrained control capabilities. Rather than producing a separate controller for each task, the framework yields a shared model for each interaction domain, supporting diverse tasks involving terrain or object interaction. Extensive experiments in simulation and the real world demonstrate that Ada-BFM enables diverse scene interaction behaviors, accelerates learning compared with training from scratch, and largely preserves pretrained behavioral competence. These findings establish Ada-BFM as an effective and efficient approach to extending the capabilities of BFMs beyond their pretraining settings while retaining their strengths in general whole-body control.

Abstract: Robots must often keep working after the world changes, as when a block moves or the camera shifts while the robot still acts on what it saw before. Recent work showed that one change during a task lowers success for every policy tested, but did not separate the effect of its timing from the cost of the changed world, or measure what it costs afterwards. We introduce CALVIN-MAX, a benchmark of 7,828 cases on CALVIN's five-instruction chains, each with one change to the objects, the camera, the robot, or the instruction. Each case compares three runs that are identical until the affected instruction begins: one without the change, one with it in place from that moment, and one in which it arrives while the robot is moving. The second run against the first gives the cost of the changed world, and the third against the second the effect of meeting it mid-motion. On 1,526 held-out cases per policy, with hypotheses fixed in advance, each of ten released policies loses 4.4 to 9.9 percentage points on the affected instruction over the eight core changes. Whether timing matters depends on the change: one that alters what the robot sees costs 5.4 points less mid-motion than from the start, whereas a change to the scene itself or to the robot shows no detectable effect. Rewinding the robot to the start of the instruction removes the benefit for every policy; the stricter test we fixed in advance, that the rewound run then matches the second run, passes for three. The cost also outlasts the affected instruction: the change stays in place, and a policy that still completes that instruction is 5.0 to 16.8 points less likely to complete the next one.

</details>

Abstract: World-action models combine action generation with future prediction, allowing visual transitions to serve as supervision for policy learning. Abstract: Robots operating in complex environments occasionally encounter situations where autonomous navigation fails. Requesting and receiving a timely intervention from their human supervisor can be critical for failure prevention or recovery. However, requesting help too frequently or at inappropriate times can increase operator workload and reduce team efficiency, while delayed requests may lead to prolonged downtime or unrecoverable failures. In addition to alert timing, the human, robot and task context also influences the intervention outcomes. This paper proposes an algorithm for learning to generate adaptive human intervention requests during human-robot teaming. A help-seeking policy is trained via offline reinforcement learning based on past intervention helpfulness and autonomous progress. This learned alert policy enables a robot to selectively request human help when the intervention outcome is likely to be beneficial. We evaluate the proposed policy in different task environments and user expertise levels in simulation, followed by evaluation in a physical user study. Our findings demonstrate that the learned policy improves exploration efficiency, reduces unnecessary alerts, and enhances novice user support without compromising expert performance in real-time human-robot teaming missions.

Abstract: Memory-dependent long-horizon manipulation requires vision-language-action (VLA) policies to infer task progress and recall evidence absent from current observations. Abstract: Robot-programming agents improve executable control policies through repeated testing and repair, yet binary success saturates while execution risk persists across unseen scenes. We introduce MARGIN, a risk-aware agent that converts successful robot rollouts into actionable evidence for program refinement. Its near-miss margin measures the fraction of the execution budget remaining in the slowest successful development rollout. The agent uses this signal to prioritize fragile programs, guide targeted repairs, and select revisions that preserve development success while increasing execution headroom. An exact safe-racing rule accelerates success-based candidate evaluation. We evaluate MARGIN across LIBERO-Pro, LIBERO-90, and Robosuite. In our 80-task LIBERO-Pro reproduction, 78 programs attain perfect development success while accounting for substantial failures on unseen seeds. The near-miss margin predicts held-out failures with an AUC of 0.774, and low-margin programs exhibit 2.97 times higher failure risk. Across two independent evaluation rounds spanning 37 unique LIBERO-Pro tasks, MARGIN reduces executed control steps by 10.7% and 15.0%, with reductions of 18.0-20.0% on LIBERO-90 and Robosuite. Safe racing reduces fixed-sequence candidate-evaluation episodes by 53.5% while retaining identical selections, and paired execution probes improve acceptance decisions by identifying hidden regressions. Together, these results establish near-miss evidence as an effective basis for risk-aware, budget-efficient robot program refinement.

Abstract: Moving the camera changes where objects appear in an image, challenging RGB-based imitation learning. A common strategy pairs synthesized views with the same demonstrated action, but ignores supporting physical correspondence across views. By predicting depth with camera calibrations, 3D policies may handle it yet suffer the issue of varied observed point sets. To address these limitations, we propose ReGeoAnch, a geometry-anchored imitation learning framework for viewpoint generalization. Specifically, we build global anchors in the robot base frame and local anchors in the gripper frame, providing shared physical references across viewpoints. Furthermore, Decoupled Appearance-Guided Geometric Readout uses Point-to-Anchor Aggregation (PAA) to obtain comparable and stable geometry around anchors beyond pointwise correspondence. A controllable and decoupled information flow, Anchor-to-Policy Readout (APR), leverages complementary visual and geometric information and supports viewpoint generalization: view-dependent appearances are queried by view-invariant robot state to select task-relevant anchors, with geometric values alone to form the final readout. We further introduce Cross-View Readout Consistency to align the directions of policy readouts from paired source and synthesized target views. Experiments on 12 manipulation tasks across MetaWorld and ManiSkill3 demonstrate improved viewpoint generalization over baseline policies.

Abstract: Recovering articulated robot state from video supports metric spatial sensing for manipulation, scene reconstruction, and action planning. Yet the problem is ill-posed: different intrinsics, camera-to-robot poses, and joint angles can explain similar observations. Abstract: Traversing complex terrain requires robots to coordinate diverse locomotion skills while adapting to unseen obstacles and changing environments. Abstract: Visual world models enable planning through predictive latent states. However, entangled agent and exogenous dynamics can obscure task progress. Disentangling visual dynamics for planning requires addressing two challenges: 1) suppressing cross-factor interference while retaining useful information; and 2) linking factors to task-relevant signals, since stability alone does not ensure meaningful planning distances. Therefore, we introduce FactorWorld, a disentangled visual dynamics framework for world model planning. To separate agent and exogenous dynamics, we propose a Counterfactual Consensus Residual Factorizer (CCRF) guided by bidirectional counterfactual pairs. CCRF separates shared context from dynamic residuals, combining average and worst-partner constraints to suppress interference with state and motion supervision to retain useful information. To translate these factors into planning decisions, we introduce semantic routing to refine full-representation task predictions for state estimation, trajectory scoring, and task-adapted planning. Experiments on MiniGrid, MetaDrive, and CARLA show improved bidirectional factor stability across two backbones. On MiniGrid, factor routing raises mean SR from 78.80% to 82.40%. Across four continuous control tasks, including Push-T, FactorWorld achieves mean success rates ranging from 91.67% to 100% across tasks. These results demonstrate that combining decoupled visual dynamics with explicit tasks can significantly improve the planning capabilities of world models.

</details>

Abstract: Time is a fundamental dimension of robotic intelligence: a robot may fail despite physically correct actions if it acts outside a valid time window, responds too slowly to an event, or fails to meet required throughput. Yet existing robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize spatial correctness and logical task completion, largely overlooking the temporal constraints of real-world applications. We present RoboClock, a unified benchmark for time-aware robotic manipulation featuring 60 tasks across five application domains. Mirroring these failure modes, RoboClock structures tasks around three types of temporal constraints: Temporal Window, Event-Reactive, and Throughput. Furthermore, RoboClock incorporates a Latency-Aligned evaluation protocol that continuously advances environment time during model inference and control-interface delays, paired with time-sensitive metrics. Evaluating over 30 methods, we find that the best-performing approach is the trained VLA, yet it achieves only 8.3% temporal success even on Easy-level tasks, with persistent challenges in generalization and transfer. These findings reveal fundamental limitations in current robotic systems and highlight the need to jointly optimize physical competence, temporal reasoning, and execution latency.

</details>

Abstract: Agent-orchestrated robot systems combine the general reasoning capability of multimodal agents with the precise physical control of learned visuomotor policies. Yet such systems often fail at the moment of handoff: a policy is reliable only from a limited set of start states, while the agent may invoke it outside this region. We call this region the policy's handoff boundary. Existing whole-task policies typically have handoff boundaries that are narrow and difficult for an agent to characterize, making reliable coordination challenging. We propose Boundary-Aware Policy Learning, which explicitly shapes and then expands the handoff boundary of a visuomotor policy. First, Boundary-Explicit Training fine-tunes the policy on contact segments with diverse start poses, yielding a wide and well-defined boundary that can be communicated to the agent through a simple handoff protocol. Second, Boundary-Expanding Self-Improvement retrains the policy on curated successful system rollouts, extending the boundary toward earlier stages of each task and allowing the agent to hand over sooner with fewer language-model calls. Across RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboDojo, our system outperforms the strongest baseline by over 26 percentage points, reaching 98.4% success in randomized RoboTwin 2.0 scenes, while further reducing its API cost and execution time by 41.3% and 37.5%, respectively. These results suggest that the key to robust agent-policy orchestration lies not only in improving either component individually, but also in explicitly shaping the boundary between them.

</details>

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) aim to construct a unified architecture capable of understanding world state evolution and guiding action generation. However, existing visual branches focus on predicting static visual observation, rather than reflecting potential transition information that captures the evolution of world states under motion interactions. This leads to representational entanglement between high-level physical condition evolution and low-level action trajectory generation within the Action Model, creating a structural bottleneck while weakening the predictive capability of world evolution modeling for action generation. We propose PILOT (Physical Inference for Latent Optimized Trajectories), whose core Representational Deduction (RD) bridges this gap by integrating motion chain-of-thought (CoT) guidance as a native model capability. Specifically, RD aims to encourage the action branch to explicitly model potential state transition tokens, which are retained as CoT in the reasoning space to guide fine-grained motion trajectory. Experiments demonstrate that RD not only improves the success rate and generalization ability of WAMs in complex robotic manipulation tasks but also enhances the model's physical interpretability by decoupling high-level motion semantics from low-level trajectory details. Furthermore, the abundant state transition supervision signals introduced by RD effectively alleviate the sparse supervision in action generation, supporting data-efficient real-robot adaptation with reduced demonstration sets.

Abstract: Composing frozen Vision-Language-Action (VLA) skills under an agentic planner promises scalable long-horizon manipulation without long-horizon demonstrations, yet strong atomic skills routinely fail at seams even under a correct plan. Standard remedies reset the robot's pose or draft entry conditions in language, missing the physical cause we uncover via causal interventions: even after re-homing, a frozen VLA rigidly retraces its habitual trajectory and collides with displaced scene residue, whether or not it can see it. We propose the Skill Envelope, a policy-measured 3D geometric precondition capturing the volume a frozen skill sweeps during successful executions. Exploiting the insight that the rigidity making a frozen skill fragile at seams also makes its trajectory predictable in advance, we expose each envelope as two classical 3D tools (CHECK to detect blocking residue and FREE to locate safe placement regions), establishing a division of labor where VLAs execute in-distribution manipulation and code repairs out-of-distribution states. On LIBERO-Goal with frozen $π_{0.5}$, our zero-failure envelope predicts collisions at 0.946 AUROC (0.944 from one RGB-D view) and beats frontier VLMs on seam readiness (0.902 vs. 0.793); using it for skill ordering and code repair lifts success from 82.8% to 92.3% on two-step seams and from 29.7% to 93.8% on drawer-bearing 3-4 step chains, matching privileged world reset. Together, these results show that reliable VLA composition hinges on measuring a policy's physical workspace rather than guessing preconditions in language. Ongoing work closes the loop with autonomous LLM orchestration, synthesized free-object recovery, and cross-policy evaluation on cluttered benchmarks and real hardware.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon robot manipulation requires more than predicting the next action from the current observation. The policy must maintain a global procedural prior that specifies the expected progress of each subtask stage, while also tracking the realized progress produced by its interactions. To this end, we propose RoboDemo, a demonstration-augmented manipulation framework built on Dual-Cascaded Test-Time-Training. RoboDemo accepts either a task-relevant human demonstration video or a semantically compatible robot video retrieved from simulation corpus as reference, allowing us to evaluate its applicability with and without paired human demonstrations. Task semantics and physical motion information from the reference video are then transferred into persistent demonstration-intent memory. During deployment, we causally compress online observations, robot states, and executed actions into interaction-progress memory. To keep reference intent stable while interaction progress continually evolves, reference and execution video tokens independently update their respective fast-weight states. The action expert performs cascaded memory reads, first obtaining reference guidance and then refining it using realized progress, allowing the policy to adapt to execution deviations without overwriting the stored intent. RoboDemo achieves 90.7% success on Imitator Game and 73.3% success across three real-world household tasks. Real-world videos, code and hf-weights are available in our project website.

Abstract: Training humanoid vision-language-action (VLA) models relies heavily on costly real-robot teleoperation. Recent efforts convert egocentric human demonstrations into humanoid training data. However, most methods still require wearable motion trackers and leave important hand-object interaction cues implicit. Many also mix in real-robot teleoperation data for VLA post-training. These dependencies limit scalable data collection, while implicit interaction cues make precise contact harder to learn. To overcome these limitations, we propose EgoLoAF, the first paradigm to derive all training data for humanoid loco-manipulation directly from egocentric demonstrations without wearable motion trackers. We post-train the VLA on the converted data alone, without real-robot teleoperation demonstrations, and use explicit hand-object interaction cues to guide action generation. Specifically, to obtain humanoid action targets, we estimate torso motion from recorded camera poses and reconstruct both hand trajectories from video. From the same recording, we use each hand's current pose relative to the object to represent hand-object affordances. During post-training, we inject these affordances into the action expert through cross-attention while keeping the vision-language backbone frozen. Additionally, we release 128,400 egocentric demonstration episodes spanning 1,000 hours and 1,284 tasks, together with converted humanoid action trajectories and rendered robot-view observations. Across four real-world tasks, EgoLoAF nearly achieves teleoperation performance (73.1 vs. 74.4 in mean Task Progress Score), while achieving a mean 4.03 times improvement in data-collection efficiency.

</details>

Abstract: We put a Thor robot on a browser voice call, with speech sent from Safari, answers played through the robot's loudspeaker and transcripts returned to the phone. We separate HTTPS signaling, relayed WebRTC media and speech generation into independently observable routes. The browser prefetches relay credentials and allows a 500 ms window after its first relay candidate. The backend starts speech and WebRTC-answer preparation concurrently within a 55 s deadline. A session slot and a non-blocking speaker lock coordinate calls with local voice access. A 2 s gate gives the controller an opportunity to suppress chat before playback. Fifteen of sixteen first dials reached ready-to-speak, comprising seven of eight Wi-Fi calls with a median of 5.57 s and eight of eight cellular calls with a median of 6.76 s. All twenty scored occupancy and recovery scenarios admitted a later call. During five 60 s outages, the backend released the line while the phone remained offline. Across 40 controlled replays, enabling the gate suppressed chat when the controller decided at 1 s. At 3 s, chat and controller feedback both played in sequence, with no overlapping output in any trace. Two additional boundary traces exposed a receipt naming a later feedback item and a new call's query returning its predecessor's feedback. Abstract: Robot in-context learning (ICL) enables a policy to perform unseen tasks by following contextual demonstrations, without task-specific fine-tuning. This is increasingly valuable as large language models reasoning at low frequency can specify tasks through demonstrations and delegate high-frequency execution to such a policy. However, methods conditioned on human videos are bounded by scarce human-robot pairs, while those conditioned on robot demonstrations accept only one or two source types at limited scale. We present Any2Act, a foundation model for robot ICL. Our key insight is that a demonstration should be expressed in the camera layout and action space of the robot that performs it (the executor), not of whoever provided it (the demonstrator). This unified embodiment-grounded representation decouples learning to follow from admitting a new source. Any2Act learns to follow once, from robot-to-robot pairs that already take this form in 1.2M real trajectories spanning 10K tasks. Human videos, simulation, UMI, and other robots are then translated into this representation, and source-routed adapters trained on only a few hundred pairs each absorb the residual gap while the pretrained model stays frozen. For long horizons, a training-free tracker feeds only the demonstration window around the executor's progress, bounding the context and rolling back to redo failed steps. From a single demonstration, Any2Act achieves 57.5% success on unseen tasks without fine-tuning, exceeding VLAs fine-tuned on 50 demonstrations per task; other demonstrators retain 74-91% of the success achieved with the executor's own demonstrations.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models can write competent robot navigation code, but not only does the research on the topic advance, a model may also, due to the volume of findings, fail to recall relevant findings and apply them to the task at hand. In this work, we test whether providing particular research papers as context improves the LLM-written navigation code for the Go2 quadruped, with the LLM (Claude Opus 5) and locomotion policy frozen. Specifically, we compare the navigation controllers the LLM writes when provided with and without particular papers across three settings: behavior trees, navigators edited from a shared base, and controllers written from scratch. When a maze's shortest route is blocked mid-episode, 2 of 8 controllers written with the context of an incremental-replanning paper complete all four trips under Isaac Sim physics, against 7 of 8 without it (two-sided Fisher's exact test, p = 0.041). This difference appears only under physics: in an idealized kinematic simulator, the controllers written with and without the paper are indistinguishable. We hypothesize that an LLM provided research papers as context tends to overweight both their content and their goals -- in this case, the efficiency of incremental replanning -- negatively affecting the fulfillment of the main task and leading to less robust code.

Abstract: Automated guided vehicles (AGVs) that transport a load together must keep a formation while each vehicle tracks its own reference, and they coordinate over wireless links with link-dependent, time-varying and jumping delays. We propose a cascade design for differential-drive AGVs modelled as kinematic unicycles: delayed data from the neighbours drive only a second-order filter that shifts each vehicle's own reference, and an unmodified gain-scheduled tracking law follows the shifted reference. A saturation keeps the shifted reference feasible, and the feed-forward is exact, so the tracking loop never sees the network. We prove that tracking and formation errors remain bounded for arbitrary delays and converge for every bounded delay and every directed, possibly switching, graph. We also construct a strict Lyapunov function with explicit constants and prove exponential convergence and a local input-to-state estimate with delay-independent gains. On undirected graphs, the coupling attenuates the formation error caused by persistent local errors by at least the factor lambda/(lambda+Lambda*lambda_2(L)) (leak lambda, coupling gain Lambda, algebraic connectivity lambda_2(L)), for any delay. Every inequality is verified with an event-driven, error-controlled simulation. A robustness study on a high-fidelity AGV model (drive dynamics, slip, localisation errors, clock offsets, lossy Wi-Fi) comprises 4,894 runs, including paired comparisons against tuned baselines. The cascade never failed, with latencies up to 4 s, 90% packet loss and up to 12 vehicles, whereas a direct delayed coupling failed when its gain saturated the drives. Coupling reduced the formation error after traction losses by 21-32% and after safety stops by up to 47%, at the price of tracking accuracy. Localisation bias and clock offsets, which no coupling law can observe, limit the achievable accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Facial robots require many coordinated, smallamplitude motions, but accommodating these motions in a compact face remains difficult. This paper presents an anatomically inspired facial-robot platform that reproduces the layered organization of the human facial musculoskeletal system. The platform comprises bone, muscle, and skin components whose geometry, shape, and attachment locations are derived from three-dimensional anatomical data. Wire-driven, compliant muscle components are routed through PTFE tubes, allowing actuation to be placed away from the face. We describe the selection and fabrication of 65 muscles (34 muscle types) required to reproduce the 47 target action units (AUs) in the Facial Action Coding System (FACS). We then evaluate the reproducibility of individual AUs in two stages: muscle actuation without skin and actuation after the silicone skin is attached. Forty of the 47 target AUs were reproduced with the muscle components, and 30 were reproduced after skin attachment. These results demonstrate the feasibility of reproducing a broad range of FACS facial expressions using an anatomically inspired musculoskeletal structure. The proposed platform provides a foundation for the systematic development and evaluation of future anatomically inspired facial robots

Abstract: Image noise can confound rotation and translation estimates in monocular visual navigation for unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) at low and moderate altitudes, where even small perturbations may cause large errors in translation direction. Inspired by angular cues in long-range visual perception, we propose rotation-only visual odometry (ROVO) for monocular aerial imagery. ROVO estimates pairwise rotations from matched images, robustly averages them over overlapping windows, and aligns consecutive windows through shared frames. By retaining only orientation in the trajectory state, it prevents uncertain landmark depth from propagating to later estimates. In trajectory simulation, sliding-window ROVO achieved a mean orientation error just 10.7% as large as that of the conventional SLAM baseline. On real flight data, ROVO achieved rotation accuracy comparable to ORB-SLAM3 and DPVO under the original Bell412 settings. Abstract: Tactile sensing is essential for contact-rich robotic manipulation, but existing visuo-tactile simulators trade physical fidelity for scalability, and benchmarks largely focus on two-finger grippers. Abstract: Driving world models anticipate action consequences through representations of how the world evolves. For autonomous driving, these representations must capture both the surrounding scene and the ego vehicle's motion under a candidate plan. We propose CodeWAM, a driving world model that incorporates executable code as part of its world representation. Code represents ego dynamics by expressing how the vehicle state evolves under plan execution, while a learned visual model captures scene-dependent consequences. Executing the code produces a candidate-conditioned motion sequence, which is evaluated relative to the prior plan and recorded motion history. The resulting execution consequences and learned scene outcomes jointly represent the candidate's future, enabling the planner to assess both what a plan may lead to and how the vehicle transitions into that future. The selected plan becomes part of the next decision's execution context, linking successive choices within the model. CodeWAM achieves 93.6 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 navtest, 92.1 EPDMS on v2 navtest, and 50.6 EPDMS on NAVHARD. Further experiments demonstrate improved pairwise execution consistency and gains on Hydra-MDP and DrivoR without additional training, supporting executable code as a complementary representation for driving world models.

To enable this broader delivery paradigm, we propose CoFiNav, a VLM-guided coarse-to-fine UAV semantic navigation framework that progressively grounds relation-defined terminal destinations through hierarchical semantic search and active 3D observation. Given a task description, a coarse prior map from OpenStreetMap (OSM), and accumulated observations, CoFiNav uses a vision-language model (VLM) to determine which unresolved entity or spatial relation should be verified next. A geometry-aware module converts the semantic observation request into feasible viewpoints using RGB images and LiDAR, while conventional motion planning handles safe execution. Experiments across multiple urban environments show that CoFiNav outperforms representative adapted baselines in success rate while requiring shorter flight paths and fewer observations.

Abstract: Cooperative transportation of heavy payloads by heterogeneous mobile manipulators requires coordinated payload motion, force distribution, and compliant physical interaction. This paper presents MRCT, an object-centric two-layer control architecture for multi-robot transportation. At the object level, a model predictive controller (MPC) generates a coordinated contact-force reference for the payload. At the robot level, whole- body impedance control (WBIC) modules on individual robots realise the reference through a confidence-based blending of velocity-tracking and impedance torques. The blending mechanism emphasises tracking when the joint-velocity error is small and increases compliance when tracking degrades because of abrupt reference changes or physical disturbances. Simulation and hardware experiments with a heterogeneous team comprising a single-arm and a dual-arm mobile manipulator transporting a near-rated table payload demonstrate accurate object tracking, regulated contact forces, and stable payload orientation. Parameter sensitivity studies, obstacle-avoidance transportation, and step-load disturbance experiments further demonstrate the effectiveness of MRCT for reliable cooperative transportation under the evaluated conditions

</details>

During deployment, the fused estimates adapt the network online. To improve both localization and this supervision, a dual-window estimator fuses vision, IMU, and leg odometry, relates loaded feet to the visually reconstructed terrain through a deformation-aware constraint, and weights the constraints by variance component estimation. AirLeg completes all 17 GrandTour sequences with the lowest average error (0.19 m) among 19 methods. Abstract: Learned driving planners keep being updated after deployment as new logs arrive, yet continual learning evaluations rarely ask whether an update improves on the deployed model, and their verdicts rest on unstated evaluation choices. We present ContinualDrive, a deployment-anchored benchmark for post-deployment continual learning of driving planners. It updates a deployed planner on real-world nuPlan logs one scenario-type group at a time, evaluates the same checkpoints in open and closed loop, makes four measurement axes explicit (evaluation loop, reference checkpoint, temporal aggregation and stream protocol), and anchors a three-valued release verdict to the deployed checkpoint. We study two planners (PlanTF and Diffusion Planner) under full fine-tuning and adapter or LoRA updates, with and without replay or gradient projection, in three task orders. Verdicts can reverse or vanish when one axis changes: open-loop ADE ranks the final PlanTF model above the deployed one in half of its core-suite runs, whereas every closed-loop score ranks it below; backward transfer misses this loss, which on average is already present when each group has just been learned; and regime rankings vary far less across training seeds than across task orders. In the core suite of full and adapter updates, no regime measurably improved on the deployed planner in closed loop; on a panel more than ten times larger every evaluated final checkpoint trails it, and joint training on all groups recovers much of the loss without exceeding its nuPlan closed-loop score. We distill these findings into a reporting contract and will release the code and all checkpoints.

Abstract: General-purpose robotic manipulation requires task reasoning, failure recovery, and precise control, yet limited manipulation data make these abilities difficult to obtain from a single policy. Recent multimodal agents show promise in task reasoning and recovery but lack reliable fine-grained control and incur high inference cost, whereas learned policies provide efficient fine-grained execution but struggle beyond their learned skills. Although their complementary strengths suggest combining the two, doing so requires repeatedly deciding which executor should act as task demands change. Instead of relying on the agent for each decision, we present Next-Manip, an on-demand agent--policy collaboration framework that decouples executor allocation from costly agent inference. We propose an Agent Invocation Controller (AIC) that allocates control between the policy and the agent through a lightweight classifier of current execution demands. We further introduce Task Dynamics Context (TDC), which packages informative manipulation changes from in-context examples into compact task references to support both agent reasoning and executor allocation. Across two simulation benchmarks and real-world experiments, Next-Manip achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing agent inference costs.

</details>

Abstract: Training and evaluating robots across diverse tasks requires a supply of simulation environments suited to those tasks. While recent scene and task generators offer a promising way to expand this supply, delivering a usable task environment still faces two practical gaps: generated scenes that look plausible in appearance often fail to support the intended task interactions or remain physically stable in simulation, and generated scoring programs frequently attempt task conditions without implementing them faithfully. We present EmbodiedStudio, a task-driven simulation generation platform that bridges these gaps by jointly authoring scene, task, robot-behavior, and rubric programs under closed-loop simulation feedback and independent review. Crucially, as the coding agent iteratively debugs scenes, behaviors, and stateful rubrics in simulation, this closed-loop process natively yields both forward construction experience for learning to build environments and physical execution experience for policy learning, all bound to frozen environment revisions for repeatable evaluation. Across scene generation, rubric evaluation, ablations, and learning studies, EmbodiedStudio substantially improves task readiness and rubric faithfulness while improving physical stability and retaining actionable supervision signals for layout authoring and policy adaptation.

Abstract: Maps are widely used in the robotics community for localization and path planning and the ability to identify incorrect or inaccurate regions of a map is an area of active research interest. For large environments, manual edits of the map can be very time consuming and a tool which can assist a map editor is needed. With this paper, we contribute to robot introspection through self-assessment of map quality, using only a given map as input to be independent of the underlying mapping algorithm. In this paper, we propose a self-supervised learning approach to map quality assessment of 2D occupancy grid maps which identifies regions of a map that require further investigation. We leverage an autoencoder for patches extracted from the map to capture the dominant structure of the map. By evaluating the reconstruction loss of a patch reconstructed with the learned model, we can evaluate whether it contains an unusual structure, which indicates the need of further investigation. We evaluate our method on the outcome of state-of-the-art grid mapping methods using benchmark datasets as well as maps from warehouse environments, highlighting the application to more complex environments. Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) offers robots a way to acquire new skills from a single demonstration without parameter updates. However, existing robotic ICL methods either rely on large-scale pretraining constrained by scarce, costly robot interaction data or require additional annotations yet struggle to generalize across tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose MXI-MemICL, a framework for memory-guided ICL in vision-language-action (VLA) models. By treating demonstrations as retrievable memory, MXI-MemICL enables in-context manipulation without additional large-scale pretraining or specialized annotations. Specifically, a visual-action causal encoder turns actions and their observed effects in demonstrations and completed interactions into reusable memory to guide execution. To retrieve experience suited to upcoming execution, an intention predictor forecasts the next stage's intent and uses it to query memory. The retrieved experience conditions the language backbone through experience-token prefixes and gated cross-attention, guiding action generation with a learned gate that regulates its influence. MXI-MemICL improves success over the baseline by +8.2% and +14.4% on RoboCasa365 and RoboTwin 2.0. Real-robot experiments show a +9.0% gain, with demonstrations adding +5.0% on seen and +16.7% on unseen tasks. The code will be made publicly available.

Abstract: Socially-aware robot person following (RPF) requires a mobile robot to follow a designated person in dynamic human environments while maintaining target identity, avoiding obstacles and pedestrians, and preserving socially comfortable formations. Existing benchmarks only partially evaluate this coupled problem: embodied visual tracking benchmarks emphasize target visibility with limited social interaction, person re-identification benchmarks study perception without closed-loop control, and motion-planning benchmarks often assume ground-truth target and pedestrian states. We introduce Follow-Bench 2.0, an end-to-end 3D benchmark for evaluating socially-aware RPF under coupled perception--planning challenges. Built in Unreal Engine, Follow-Bench 2.0 provides difficulty-leveled scenarios with diverse pedestrian flows, lighting and weather conditions, cluttered layouts, bottlenecks, queueing behaviors, distractors, and temporary target occlusions. The benchmark supports modular perception--planning pipelines, end-to-end visual policies, and foundation-model-based active trackers under a unified closed-loop protocol. It further evaluates both back- and side-following configurations and reports safety--comfort metrics covering task success, target visibility, target recovery, social-space intrusion, following formation, and motion smoothness. Experiments show that RPF-oriented modular pipelines substantially outperform current end-to-end visual policies on close and socially comfortable following, but also reveal that socially-aware RPF remains far from solved in interaction-heavy environments and especially in side-following. Follow-Bench 2.0 therefore provides a diagnostic platform for studying how perception errors, occlusions, crowd interactions, and following configurations jointly affect robot person following.

</details>

Abstract: Teleoperation of mobile robots represents a critical capability for operations in hazardous environments, yet maintaining adequate situational awareness remains challenging without effective visualization and control interfaces. This work presents a multi-modal extended reality (XR) system for robot teleoperation and scene exploration that integrates immersive visual and haptic feedback modalities. To address motion sickness commonly induced by 360-degree camera feeds in head-mounted displays (HMDs), the system employs a fixed dual-camera configuration: a forward-facing camera for primary navigation and a rear-facing camera for enhanced situational awareness without operator discomfort. For navigation in confined or low-visibility conditions, a 16-channel Velodyne LiDAR generates real-time 2D occupancy grid maps, which are converted into vibrational haptic feedback on handheld controllers to provide intuitive, non-visual obstacle detection. User evaluation with 20 participants demonstrates that this haptic modality significantly reduces the cognitive load associated with obstacle avoidance during teleoperation.

Abstract: Collecting diverse real-world manipulation demonstrations is costly, motivating the use of simulation to augment existing demonstrations for robot policy learning. When objects, layouts, or robots change, transferring motions from a source demonstration can produce behavior that is semantically implausible or physically infeasible in the new setting. We introduce RoboRecast, an agentic optimization framework that preserves the interactions required by the source task while jointly optimizing the simulated scene and robot motion to make those interactions executable in the target setting. When an interaction fails, the agent uses simulation feedback to diagnose the cause, revise the relevant scene or motion parameters, and test whether the revision resolves the failure. This flexibility allows RoboRecast to generate synthetic data from both human and robot demonstrations and to adapt them to new layouts, objects, and robot embodiments. When target demonstrations are available, adding RoboRecast data improves policy success on both real-world single-arm and simulated bimanual tasks. Even without demonstrations in the target configuration, RoboRecast adapts source demonstrations to support policy learning with new objects or robot embodiments.

Abstract: Online reinforcement learning (RL) enables vision-language-action policies to improve through environmental interaction. However, in long-horizon manipulation, sparse success/failure feedback provides little information about where policies encounter bottlenecks. Moreover, repeated full-task rollouts also leave these bottlenecks weakly analyzed and insufficiently practiced. To this end, we introduce ReFocus, a progress-guided multi-round RL framework that alternates between Global and Local RL to identify task-specific bottlenecks and allocate additional practice to them. Specifically, during Global RL, we decompose each task into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of subtasks. Then a DAG-based Progress Reward is leveraged for both policy training and bottleneck diagnosis. Based on the identified bottlenecks, ReFocus selects Adaptive Practice Windows, defining task segments from an entry stage to a target stage. It allocates additional interaction to these segments during Local RL while updating the entire policy. After that, it evaluates the policy to perform the full task for the next round of RL, forming an iteration between Global and Local RL. Experiments on LIBERO-Long and RoboCasa365 show that ReFocus achieves success rates of 97.8% and 51.3%, respectively, compared with 90.6% and 39.3% for the $π_{0.5}$ baseline. Furthermore, we evaluate a real-world adaptation on three long-horizon tasks. On BreadBaker, ReFocus achieves 95% success, compared with 30% for the $π_{0.5}$. Project page: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.

Abstract: Dynamic obstacle avoidance is essential for safely deploying humanoid robots in real-world environments, where rapidly approaching objects require immediate evasive responses. Recent advances in reflexive evasion have focused primarily on quadrupeds, which typically rely on large-scale whole-body maneuvers. In contrast, humanoids can exploit their rich body articulation to evade through small, localized body adjustments. By moving threatened body regions without repositioning the entire body, these responses may require less execution time and less surrounding free space. However, local adjustments can alter mass distribution or support conditions, making it challenging to achieve rapid evasion while maintaining whole-body stability. To this end, we propose BARE (Body-Aware Reflexive Evasion), a humanoid reflexive evasion framework guided by human motion priors that enables rapid local evasion while maintaining whole-body stability. A risk-guided motion adapter is designed to select reference motions from a retargeted human evasive motion library based on the threatened body region and to adjust their temporal phases based on collision urgency, allowing the robot to reach evasive postures before potential contact. Conditioned on the adapted reference and link-level collision risk, a reflexive evasion policy learns through reinforcement learning to coordinate local body adjustments with whole-body stabilization. We validate BARE in simulation and real-world experiments, demonstrating its ability to perform targeted local evasion while maintaining whole-body stability.

</details>

Abstract: Robot demonstrations provide more than actions to imitate: they reveal how actions change the world and how those changes contribute to task success. Recent world-action models (WAMs) extend vision-language-action (VLA) models with the first signal by jointly predicting actions and future observations, yet this predictive ability serves only as an auxiliary loss for policy learning. Meanwhile, most policy improvement methods learn dynamics and values in separate models, increasing training overhead and limiting representation sharing between these complementary objectives. We introduce DexWAC, a unified world-actor-critic model that jointly learns an actor, an action-conditioned world model, and a bootstrapped critic in a single robot pretraining stage. Our key insight is that visual prediction encourages control-relevant features, while value supervision promotes task-relevant representations; together, they shape a representation suited to policy learning. Built on an image-editing backbone, DexWAC predicts, for each action chunk, the visual latent and proprioception at its endpoint together with its reward and continuation, so the world model alone efficiently yields complete imagined transitions without generating intermediate video frames. After adapting the world model and critic on real rollouts, we improve the pretrained actor through either PPO on imagined rollouts or critic-guided best-of-$N$ selection. DexWAC surpasses the large-scale pretrained VLA and WAM baselines in simulation and achieves 75% average success on two real-world tasks, compared with 45% for $π_{0.5}$. Its world model is more accurate and faster than video-based baselines, and ablations confirm that learning dynamics and value together improves the policy.
