Abstract: Initial attitude alignment between local navigation frames and the Earth-centered Earth-fixed (ECEF) frame is a critical prerequisite for multi-sensor integrated navigation, such as Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS)/inertial/odometry fusion, before global and local measurements can be jointly processed. While GNSS Doppler-based alignment eliminates the need for carrier-phase ambiguity resolution or multi-antenna hardware, solving this non-convex problem via local nonlinear least squares (NLS) suffers from local minima, whereas direct semidefinite programming (SDP) relaxation incurs high latency and omits local covariance outputs. This study systematically compares NLS and SDP on a unified maximum-likelihood Doppler objective across Monte Carlo simulations and three real-world driving datasets. We demonstrate that attaching a modular dual SDP certifier to a standard manifold NLS solver recovers the global accuracy of direct SDP within 0.001 degrees, reduces real-world 95th-percentile yaw errors from over 86 degrees down to 2.88 degrees to 4.70 degrees, and cuts median solve times by up to an order of magnitude while preserving native Jacobian covariances. We conclude that candidate-driven certification bridges local estimation and global optimization due to two structural advantages: (1) our theoretical comparison reveals that SDP relaxation enlarges the feasible region while preserving the lifted cost, allowing Lagrangian duality to verify whether a fixed NLS candidate attains the global lower bound without invoking a full SDP solver, and (2) the candidate-driven architecture acts as a non-intrusive module that plugs directly into existing navigation pipelines without replacing the local estimator, preserving native tangent-space covariance outputs and low typical latency.

</details>

Abstract: A small ground robot can carry one camera and a few watts of compute, but the depth foundation models that could turn that camera into a range sensor are vision transformers sized for GPUs. On 1,000 stereo-verified points of a real outdoor drive it is not resolvably different from the vendor's Depth Anything V2-Small build, while running faster (27.1 vs 21.6 FPS). Ranging obstacles by where they meet the floor needs only the camera height, so mapping, hybrid-A* planning and SLAM run on the Pi's own CPU. Abstract: Reconstructing a scene for physics simulation requires recovering its spatial layout and the support relationships that govern its behavior. We present Support2Sim, a Real2Sim system that reconstructs simulation-ready scenes from a single RGB image. Our design objective is to maximize source fidelity, support realization, and stability within a practical time budget for scene generation. Support2Sim infers a support graph, which a contact-guided pose initializer uses to propose poses for objects with multiple supports. The initializer generates and ranks candidates by geometric fit and source-image agreement. A coarse-to-fine refiner allocates a fixed search budget across supported objects and evaluates pose updates with physics rollouts. After an accepted update, it searches from the new pose at a finer translation and rotation scale. We benchmark our method on 50 scenes spanning four datasets. Compared with the strongest baseline, Support2Sim improves source-mask IoU after 10 seconds by 21.4 pp, realizes 87.1% versus 73.2% of intended supports, and reduces mean object drift from 79.6 to 45.3 mm. Abstract: Maritime Autonomous Surface Ships (MASS) must demonstrate safety before and during operations, necessitating online risk assessment to guide decision-making at sea. This paper presents a risk-informed Rapidly Exploring Random Tree Star (RRT*) path planner that integrates a Bayesian Belief Network (BBN) as an online risk model. The BBN evaluates the probability and severity distribution of hazardous events, which are aggregated into a bounded scalar risk index. During the search, this risk index is evaluated at sample points along candidate edges. The planner optimizes a composite cost function comprising path distance, heading change penalties, and accumulated risk, subject to a hard constraint enforcing a risk acceptance limit. Each search tree vertex stores the BBN posterior at its state, enabling the tree to function as an explainable risk map for individual hazards. We define design requirements for the BBN to serve as the risk model in this context and introduce a runtime model that translates replanning deadlines into strict limits on single risk calculation times. The planner is demonstrated in a simulated harbor entry scenario involving collision and grounding hazards. Measured run times validate that the planner operates within the computational bounds predicted by the runtime model for a single replanning cycle.

</details>

Abstract: Video world models for robots are increasingly conditioned on a simulated or rendered proxy of the scene, on the assumption that the video will show what the proxy shows. Testing this assumption requires a proxy whose state is explicit and can be perturbed. We present SimPWM (Simulator-as-Proxy World Model): given a calibrated camera, it represents, from the first frame alone, the robot by its 3D model and the objects the gripper may touch as 3D points; a rule-based simulator with no trained parameters moves them under the actions, and a video model renders the result. SimPWM returns the realistic video together with the simulated state. We then freeze the simulated objects while the arm moves as recorded and check whether the video still shows the object lifted. On DROID episodes from unseen setups, the answer differs for the arm and for the objects. With each of three base video models, the arm follows the proxy (robot IoU 0.75-0.78, against at most 0.69 for end-to-end models); rendering the robot alone already gives this. What the simulated objects add is the object state: against a robot-only proxy, they cut missed pick-ups from 54% to 38%, and the video follows them only with some base models: on rigid objects, freezing them lowers the lift rate of MiniMax-H3, fine-tuned on episodes whose simulation agrees with the real video, from 42% to 10%, but that of LTX-2.3, trained on the same episodes, only from 57% to 49%, as if it inferred the grasp from the arm motion; more than half of the remaining lifts of MiniMax-H3 are of cloth and rope, which the simulator carries rigidly. With any base model, the simulated state records what the simulation did; under perturbed actions, a video that disagrees with it reveals that the video ignores the simulated state.

Abstract: Most imitation policies that predict chunks of future actions use the dense action representation, in which the chunk holds one control target per step. In stage-structured manipulation, where a task can be divided into stages with definable endpoints, the keypose action representation instead sets each step of the chunk to the endpoint of the stage that step belongs to, and a speed-capped servo supplies the motion. We compare the two in several simulated manipulation tasks and on a real robot, with the same chunk length and matched demonstrations, observations, policy architectures and update budgets; in simulation, each one's execution rule is selected on tuning scenes. When the simulated demonstrations are slowed, paused and rerouted, dense success falls sharply while keypose stays roughly level, across three policy architectures and with endpoints extracted from the recorded demonstrations; keypose also leads under object-size and position shift. With clean demonstrations, keypose leads only when they are scarce and is otherwise close to or behind dense across the simulated tasks, provided that the demonstrations and execution rule suit its executor; when they do not, it falls far below dense. The same dependence on the executor appeared on a real robot learning to open a drawer from human demonstrations: dense succeeded more often, and keypose improved substantially only after its executor's rotation and forward speed were adjusted to the demonstrations. These results suggest that keypose trades sensitivity to such variation in the demonstrations for sensitivity to execution that departs from them.

Abstract: We present the Screw Attention Policy, a geometry-centric manipulation framework that supplies task geometry explicitly and learns only execution. The policy operates on object poses, relative SE(3) transforms, and joint screw axes provided by perception and robot models. The policy requires only 30K learned parameters. Abstract: Recent advances in multi-robot manipulation have shown how robots can collaborate to accomplish complex tasks. However, successful coordination does not reveal which robots are actually needed. The same task may require different teams as robot capabilities change, resources become unavailable, or objects replace robotic support. We argue that reliable multi-robot manipulation requires physical necessity reasoning: understanding what a task demands, which resources can fulfill those demands, and which are truly necessary. We introduce MRBench, a benchmark for physical necessity reasoning in heterogeneous multi-robot manipulation. It comprises eight tasks with distinct concurrency and reach constraints, each evaluated under five configurations that change the required resources while preserving the task goal. Our evaluation reveals that existing coordination agents perform well with reference teams but struggle when robot embodiments change or passive objects can replace robotic support. To address this gap, we propose MRScissors, which separates task requirements from measured physical capabilities and identifies minimal feasible teams through constrained search. Across two model backbones, MRScissors achieves 85.4% and 91.2% team-selection accuracy, outperforming the strongest baselines by 10.4 and 9.5 points, respectively. Together, these findings expose a critical gap between coordinating robots and understanding their physical necessity.

</details>

Abstract: Discrete skill policies execute motions drawn from a finite vocabulary. In most current methods, this vocabulary is a latent codebook learned jointly with a selector and a decoder. When such a policy fails in closed loop, the failure cannot be traced to the vocabulary, to the choice of skill or to its execution. We present Movement Prototype Quantization (MPQ), which places a library of demonstrated motions after a trained policy instead of inside it. MPQ replaces each predicted action chunk with its nearest library element, corrects that element toward the prediction within a bound, and trains no parameters. Across six architectures, including a 7B vision-language-action model, the wrapped policy is statistically equivalent to the original. A reference policy executed through the library is therefore a baseline for any selector over the same library. Substituting a selector's components one at a time attributes its shortfall to them. On LIBERO-90, with selectors on three backbones, quantization costs nothing and the choice of skill costs at most 2 points under the reference's placement and correction. The selectors' own placement and correction account for nearly all of the remaining gap, up to 12 points. Offline reconstruction predicts none of these differences and misjudges library size, design and transfer across benchmark suites. Project page: https://intuitive-robots.github.io/mpq.

Abstract: In vision-based teleoperation of robotic hands, the operator receives no haptic feedback and therefore struggles to regulate the grasp force, while adding tactile sensors to the fingers increases wiring, cost and integration complexity. Compliant hands offer an alternative, as their mechanical compliance, usually treated only as a passive property for safe and adaptive grasping, can also serve as a source of information. Here, we show that the elastic transmission of a compliant, tendon-driven anthropomorphic hand can itself act as the sensor for shared-control telemanipulation. Series springs in the tendon routing are read by potentiometers in a forearm-mounted motor box, from which the hand estimates its joint states and finger forces without any external sensors. Building on this self-sensing, we develop a contact-aware shared-control framework for VR-based teleoperation. The robot hand is controlled through a mapping from the operator's hand kinematics until a contact is detected proprioceptively. Control then switches to a finger-level force controller whose set point is determined by the operator's grasping speed, and control authority can be allocated to individual fingers. Experiments validate the system's force estimation and increased grasp success rate compared with direct teleoperation for fragile and heavy objects. These results show that compliance can serve not only for passive adaptation but also as an intrinsic sensing resource for physical human-robot collaboration.

</details>

Abstract: Whole-body motion tracking enables humanoid robots to reproduce diverse skills from human demonstrations. Existing model-free reinforcement learning methods either distill motion specialists into a single policy at the cost of performance or train a generalist policy directly using cluster-scale computation. SuperMimic reduces this computational cost through a differentiable world model that implicitly learns the physics shared across motions by predicting the next state from the current state and action. However, this implicit formulation may not fully capture the underlying physical processes. Errors around changes of support can accumulate over imagined rollouts used to train the tracking policy. To address this challenge, we introduce DynaMimic, a differentiable world model that explicitly incorporates shared physics into state prediction. Specifically, its network predicts a contact wrench at each candidate support link and a residual generalized force. DynaMimic then uses these predictions to compute the next state through closed-form Lagrangian forward dynamics. Across 60 full LAFAN1 motions, DynaMimic reduces mean per-joint position error (MPJPE) by 18.6% relative to SuperMimic. For terrain tracking, we give both methods the same local height maps. Relative to terrain-conditioned SuperMimic, DynaMimic reduces MPJPE by 14.9% on A3 Parkour (66.15 to 56.28~mm) and by 10.8% on GRAIL terrain clips (141.88 to 126.57~mm). On a real A3 humanoid, the learned policies can perform diverse skills, such as floor recovery, cartwheel and a box vault.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Models (VLMs) used in robot task planning to derive assembly procedures from existing manuals may correctly interpret individual assembly actions yet confuse exact part identities, predict unsupported Part IDs, or produce inconsistent parts across assembly steps. Thus, we propose CAPER (Capacity-Aware Part-grounding and Evidence Refinement), a manual-guided robotic assembly framework that enables VLMs to generate assembly procedures with two modules--PartGround and PlanRefine. PartGround aligns step-level manual evidence with product-specific inventory and part-reference information to identify exact Part IDs and quantities, transforming free-form hardware prediction into evidence-guided assignments represented in a shared Assembly Intermediate Representation (Assembly IR). PlanRefine audits PartGround assignments against finite inventory by removing the lowest-confidence assignments. Uncertain identity cases remain marked for review in the Assembly IR. We evaluate CAPER on the IKEA Assembly in the Wild (IAW) dataset. Experiment results show that CAPER reaches a Finite-inventory Consistency Rate of 83.33% and produces zero observed out-of-inventory Part-ID occurrences. At the plan level, PlanRefine removes all observed inventory-overuse pairs (9 -> 0) and excess units (51 -> 0). Overall, the results indicate that PartGround and PlanRefine improve part reliability, while also enhancing finite-inventory consistency and local prediction accuracy. Abstract: Bathymetry measurement and estimation is critical for understanding the structure of underwater environments. While multibeam echosounders (MBES) provide accurate depth measurements, their deployment on surface vessels limits spatial resolution in deep water. Sidescan sonar (SSS), in contrast, can be deployed on autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) to operate closer to the seafloor, enabling high-resolution acoustic imaging. However, the inherent ambiguity in measurements and the limited observation coverage of sonar datasets degrade the performance of state-of-the-art methods for underwater bathymetry estimation from SSS. In this paper, we propose a differentiable neural rendering framework for SSS that enables effective estimation of bathymetry from SSS measurements. Our approach incorporates a measurement model based on SSS geometry, explicitly accounting for its wide elevation aperture and associated ambiguities. We evaluate our method on both synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating improved robustness and accuracy compared to alternative approaches.

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) build policies on pretrained video-generation models, on the premise that predicting how a scene evolves helps determine how to act. However, generating future scenes is expensive for real-time control. This motivates efficient proxies for future imagination, but how to train such proxies without sacrificing action-prediction quality remains unclear. An effective proxy need not generate a high-resolution frame; it only needs to provide enough information to distinguish between possible futures. This leads to the design principle behind iWAM: rather than denoising an entire future frame, can we condition the action policy on intermediate features from a single video-model evaluation? We show that the answer is yes. Exploiting the reversible dynamics of flow-matching models, we invert each training future into the corresponding latent noise vector. However, a single velocity evaluation at this latent does not necessarily yield informative features for action prediction. We therefore learn a bidirectional flow map that amortizes future-generation computation, enabling a single model evaluation to provide effective conditioning features. At inference, we sample a latent noise vector from the prior and condition the policy using just one video-backbone forward pass. Across simulated benchmarks and real robots, iWAM recovers the generalization benefits of WAMs while substantially reducing its inference cost.

Abstract: A single policy that controls diverse robots must not only transfer task knowledge across embodiments, but also adapt its behavior to each robot's capabilities. Existing approaches facilitate transfer through shared action representations, but these representations do not necessarily capture how differences in robot morphology should shape manipulation strategies. We introduce OmniAct, a cross-embodiment manipulation policy that conditions directly on each robot's kinematics in a specific scene to produce embodiment-specific behavior. This kinematic graph represents the robot's joint positions, motion axes, and connectivity to its visual observation, allowing the policy to account for how its embodiment related to objects and surfaces in the scene. A flow-matching policy predicts relative joint motions for each node, enabling control across robots with different degrees of freedom and kinematic configurations. To learn how physical structure shapes behavior at scale, we procedurally design and filter one million physically feasible robot embodiments; next, we develop algorithms to generate robot trajectories in simulation for all embodiments; finally, we train across diverse simulated manipulation tasks. On 2,000 embodiment-scene-held-out evaluation episodes, OmniAct achieves 63.0% success, compared to 17.8% for pi 0.5 and 57.9% for the strongest baseline; on the task families GPT-6 Astra was evaluated on, it achieves 64.7% against GPT-6 Astra's 41.1%. On a previously unseen real-world robot, it achieves nearly twice the success rate of the next best method, MolmoAct2. Beyond task success, we observe that OmniAct learns distinct manipulation strategies that exploit the capabilities and accommodate the limitations of different morphologies. ### [92] [Irreducible Governance Structure for Autonomous Agent Systems: Fair Allocation, Strategy-Proofness, and Multi-Scale Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31284)

Abstract: Autonomous agent governance is commonly framed as a problem of enforcing correct execution. However, in multi-agent systems, an equally fundamental question precedes execution: which agents are allowed to act.
  In this paper, we show that allocation is not an auxiliary concern but a structural component of governance. When multiple agents compete for execution under constrained resources, any allocation mechanism introduces unavoidable trade-offs between fairness, efficiency, and resistance to strategic manipulation. We formalize this through a set of impossibility results, including Sybil amplification and the incompatibility of desirable allocation properties.
  We then show that these allocation constraints cannot be resolved within a single layer of control. Instead, they propagate across the system, interacting with execution atomicity, state enforcement, and behavioral drift. This leads to a multi-scale governance structure composed of four orthogonal dimensions: temporal (atomicity), state (enforcement), behavioral (drift monitoring), and population (allocation).
  Our main result is an irreducibility theorem: under finite observability, no governance architecture can collapse these dimensions without loss of correctness or stability. Allocation, in particular, acts as a first-class constraint that prevents simplification of the governance stack.
  We validate these results through a series of analytical constructions and empirical ablations, showing that removing or approximating any layer leads to systematic failure modes.
  This work reframes agent governance as a structural problem: not only how actions are executed, but how access to execution is determined, and why these decisions impose irreducible architectural requirements.

</details>

We present MOZAIC, a paired benchmark with a 4-way label in which every source clip is released in all four combinations of real or fake video and real or fake audio so each modality contribution can be measured directly. Fakes cover inner-face swapping, whole-frame video diffusion and voice conversion. MOZAIC has 137,745 clips from 15,014 source pairs and four tasks: T1 AV authenticity, T2 manipulation type, T3 identity verification and T4 explanation. Overall, the best MLLM reaches only 35.6% on the four-way T1 and catches far more video-only fakes than voice-only fakes (43.6% vs 7.5%). Abstract: In recent years, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved significant progress in chart-to-code generation. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on static charts, overlooking interactive charts that enable richer and more expressive data exploration. Unlike static chart-to-code generation, which emphasizes visual consistency, interactive chart-to-code evaluation targets behavioral consistency, requiring chart states to evolve correctly under sequences of user interactions, thereby rendering traditional one-step evaluation paradigms ineffective. To address this gap, we introduce ChartInt, the first benchmark specifically designed for interactive chart-to-code generation. ChartInt consists of 2,779 carefully annotated examples covering common interaction patterns such as hover, highlight, and tooltips. Additionally, ChartInt incorporates editing tasks such as data updates, style adjustments, and cross-chart style transfer to expand real-world application scenarios. To enable reliable evaluation of interaction behavior, we propose a preset state snapshot protocol that predefines target interaction states and deterministically triggers interaction events, transforming dynamic behaviors into reproducible evaluation targets. Based on this protocol, we design a multi-dimensional evaluation framework measuring semantic and stylistic consistency. Experiments show that while current MLLMs perform well on static charts, their performance drops significantly on interactive tasks, highlighting the need for interaction-aware benchmarks.

Abstract: LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.

</details>

Abstract: Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.

</details>

Abstract: Type 1 diabetes (T1D) requires lifelong insulin therapy, and reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promise for automated dosing in the UVA/Padova simulator. However, the decisions of RL controllers remain difficult to interpret. We present XRL4T1D, a post-hoc explainability framework for evaluating pretrained insulin-dosing policies without retraining. Each policy uses 60 minutes of continuous glucose monitor (CGM) and administered-insulin history, sampled every 5 minutes, to recommend insulin. We use SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) to compare the importance of CGM and insulin-history inputs and summarize insulin-history attributions over three 20-minute windows motivated by insulin-on-board. We assess whether these attributions correspond to policy-output sensitivity through targeted input replacement and compare temporal rankings with TimeSHAP. In all 80 patient-policy-mask-size cases, replacing the highest-attribution inputs changes the normalized insulin recommendation more than replacing the lowest-attribution or random inputs. The highest-attribution insulin-history window is also the most output-sensitive in 115 of 120 patient-policy-scenario configurations, and KernelSHAP and TimeSHAP select the same top window in 111 of 120. Applying XRL4T1D to PPO and G2P2C across 20 virtual patients, we find that both controllers rely predominantly on CGM history, especially G2P2C. However, the distribution of insulin-history importance across recent, middle, and older windows varies by cohort, explainer, and analysis settings, without a consistent separation between controllers. Global feature-attribution rankings remain stable under the tested observation corruptions. These results show why attribution fidelity, temporal reproducibility, and controller-comparison robustness should be assessed separately.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion multimodal large language models rely on confidence to decide when to commit candidate tokens during generation. In multimodal tasks, however, confidence cannot distinguish whether a candidate originates from image evidence or the language prior. We perform paired forward ablation and find that nearly nine out of ten high-confidence commits are insensitive to the image, while degenerate and hallucination samples exhibit significant increases in the entropy of deep-layer attention organization. Based on this diagnosis, we propose CD-APC, a training-free decoding method. CD-APC performs evidence-adjusted scoring within a base-supported pool and uses dual gating based on model acceptability and contrastive concentration to decide whether to commit, together with an inverse-window mechanism that adapts to inter-step difficulty. Across two backbones and five benchmarks, CD-APC consistently improves performance, indicating that stable visual evidence improves model outputs.

Abstract: The growing autonomy of AI agents in using external tools and making decisions has created an urgent need for comprehensive safety benchmarks. Existing benchmarks test compromised tool channels or adaptive multi-turn attacks, though they may not capture the unique capabilities of policy-governed tool-use agents, which must resist unauthorized requests without abandoning the legitimate work embedded in the same interaction. To fill this gap, we introduce TACT-Bench, a stateful benchmark built around scenario-specific stories, personas, manipulation tactics, and response-adaptive escalation. Each adversarial task targets an action explicitly prohibited by the policy provided to the assistant, while the policy remains hidden from the attacking user. TACT pairs each adversarial task with at least one benign counterpart, and its adversarial tasks retain authorized ground-truth actions when a compliant resolution remains available. We define these base tasks as TACT-Base. We additionally introduce TACT-TrustPrime, which places an independent benign task before an unrelated attack in the same conversation. We also propose Paired Selective Trustworthiness (PST), which combines explicit safe refusal with demonstrated capability on matched benign tasks, to analyze an agent's overcautiousness and practicality. Averaged across 6 different models as assistants, TACT-Base raises Attack-Induced Failure Rate (AFR) by 17.53% over four compatible attack baselines, and TACT-TrustPrime raises it by a further 17.01%. The two evaluated defense methods reduce AFR by only 8.35%--9.41% on average and do not consistently improve PST, underscoring the need for stronger policy-enforcement methods.

Abstract: Scientific measurements reported in scholarly articles are often embedded in compound figures rather than available as machine-readable data. Recovering these measurements at literature scale requires an integrated workflow for figure localization, panel decomposition, chart identification, numerical reconstruction, and experimental context association. We present \textit{SciFig2Data}, an automated, high-throughput workflow for harvesting structured experimental data from scientific PDF repositories. The system integrates multi-scale document element detection, adaptive compound figure decomposition, two-stage OCR-based semantic filtering, Swin-based chart classification, and instruction-guided multimodal large language model (MLLM) reconstruction. It further incorporates directory-level batch execution, caching, and structured logging to support large-scale processing. We demonstrate the workflow on two biomedical literature corpora comprising 605 toxicology and pharmacology articles and 551 lipid nanoparticle (LNP) articles. The pipeline successfully reconstructed 468 and 242 tabular outputs from the respective corpora, illustrating its practical applicability to literature-scale scientific data harvesting. By associating reconstructed measurements with caption-derived experimental metadata, SciFig2Data provides a workflow for transforming graphical evidence in scholarly publications into structured, context-enriched data records for downstream AI4Science applications.

Abstract: Realistic modeling of human-driven vehicle (HDV) interactions is essential for automated driving safety testing in urban areas. Existing methods struggle to reproduce realistic safety-critical interactions involving high collision risk for the following reasons: they often assume full observability, overlooking interactions arising from occlusion. Driving styles are mainly characterized by vehicle maneuvers, limiting the diversity of safety-critical interaction behaviors. Moreover, learning signals from safety-critical interactions can be diluted by non-critical driving behaviors. To address these issues, we propose a generative AI agent to emulate interactive HDVs. The agent incorporates field-of-view modeling to account for driver visibility under occlusion and characterizes the interaction process using five metrics spanning its initial, intermediate, and final stages. It automatically learns diverse interaction styles through a dynamic mixture-of-experts network. Through residual reinforcement learning, the agent further refines generated trajectories to match the real-world distributions of safety-critical interaction characteristics. Experiments on the INTERACTION dataset show that our method reduces Jensen--Shannon divergence (JSD) by 39.9\%--87.1\% across five interaction characteristics compared with a state-of-the-art baseline. Our method achieves greater improvements of up to 87.1\% in final-stage interaction metrics, suggesting that it can reduce error accumulation in long-horizon trajectory generation. Ablation studies confirm the effectiveness of each proposed component, particularly highlighting the importance of residual refinement. Generalization experiments show that our method approaches the performance of full training on unseen intersections with only one epoch of fine-tuning.

</details>

Abstract: Spiking neural networks for multimodal audio-visual classification reach high accuracy by adopting deep-learning machinery: multi-stage convolutional or transformer encoders trained end-to-end with surrogate-gradient backpropagation through time. These prior works typically rely on full-precision (32-bit) parameters and do not treat hardware efficiency as a primary design objective. In this paper, we propose NeuTNN-AV, a much simpler hardware-amenable multimodal substrate built on the recently proposed NeuroAI Temporal Neural Network (NeuTNN) and demonstrate that it can match or outperform state-of-the-art SNN baselines with a fraction of their synapse counts. NeuTNN neurons carry multiple active dendritic segments. Each input line spikes at most once (temporal coding) and every weight is learned via a local reward-modulated spike-timing dependent plasticity (STDP) rule. All weights are 4-bit integers and inference uses only comparisons and additions. On CREMA-D, it reaches 78.6% with 2.34M synapses establishing a new state-of-the-art SNN accuracy, against 77.14% for MISNet-XL (31M parameters). On UrbanSound8K-AV, NeuTNN reaches 98.82% with 1.38M synapses, against 96.85% for SMMT (9.99M parameters) and 98.96% for MISNet-XL (31M parameters). Weight memory is 23-106x smaller than the spiking baselines, and the measured synaptic energy per inference is two to three orders of magnitude lower. Furthermore, the proposed NeuTNN learns CREMA-D in a single pass with 73.8% accuracy and demonstrates rapid online continual learning by adapting to unseen actors from a few dozen clips.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models must process continuous streams of visual observations, and the resulting computational cost limits real-time robotic control. Token pruning reduces this cost, but a fixed semantic scale of perceptual representation can overlook information needed at different stages of manipulation. We show that perceptual semantics can guide effective token selection without relying on internal attention when the selection scale adapts to the scene's evolving semantic structure. Based on this observation, we introduce TauPrune-VLA, a training-free method that controls the scale of the retained perceptual representation through a single perception temperature $τ$. To adapt this scale as manipulation proceeds, TauPrune-VLA updates $τ$ online using trends in perceptual semantics, with robot-state changes and action magnitude regulating the adjustment strength. While these semantics evolve over the course of manipulation, they remain relatively stable between consecutive frames. This continuity allows retention indices computed from the current observation to be applied at the next policy invocation, enabling token selection to run asynchronously with inference and reducing pruning overhead. Evaluated across diverse VLA models and simulation benchmarks, TauPrune-VLA achieves up to a $1.86\times$ inference speedup while maintaining near-baseline task success rates. We further validate its effectiveness on two real-world robotic platforms.

</details>

Abstract: Flow-based diffusion models have become a mainstream paradigm for text-to-image generation, where task-specific post-training produces specialist models with complementary strengths. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (OPD) offers an efficient way to consolidate these specialists into a single unified student. Recent methods strengthen supervision through teacher extrapolation, constructing stronger proxy teachers that allow distilled students to surpass the original specialists. However, their extrapolation directions remain defined solely by frozen teacher and reference models, leaving the student's own training progress unexploited. To address this limitation, we introduce DexOPD, a dual-extrapolation on-policy distillation method for diffusion models. DexOPD retains extrapolated teacher supervision while leveraging a lagging historical student as the reference for student self-extrapolation. Abstract: World-action models connect visual prediction with executable robot control. We present EffectWAM, which develops this connection through three complementary components: Effect, camera positional encoding (CaPE), and Action Grounding. Effect represents visual change as the difference between independently encoded future observations and a shared current-observation anchor. CaPE conditions endpoint pooling on relative camera pose when reliable extrinsics are available, while sharing the same learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories. Action Grounding trains the visual representation to retain measured changes in end-effector pose and gripper state. An exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets. A shared generation expert learns to jointly predict Effect and an executable action chunk through flow matching, and reuses the sampled pair to generate future video on demand. This allows the controller to execute actions without waiting for video synthesis. We train on more than 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data, excluding unused chain-of-thought data, and accommodate dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations. Our evaluation uses the Small (8B) configuration on all five benchmarks and additionally evaluates the Large (26B) configuration on RoboDojo. Experiments examine closed-loop manipulation across complementary benchmarks, and comparisons across multiple baseline policies show consistent improvements from Action Grounding.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) strengthens model capabilities through feedback from humans and environments. However, emerging evidence suggests that reward optimization can produce outputs whose errors are harder for humans to detect. To address this issue, we construct ITHV (the Image-Text Hallucination Verifiability Dataset), comprising 2,728 visually grounded instances that distinguish between obvious hallucinations, which humans readily detect, and elusive hallucinations, which humans struggle to identify. We then propose DAPO-V, an extension of DAPO that maximizes correctness subject to a budget on verifiability-aware risk. Specifically, DAPO-V combines a verifiability-aware reward with adaptive dual control, where larger penalties are assigned to incorrect responses on questions with lower human verification accuracy. An adaptive multiplier further adjusts the penalty strength according to risk-budget violations. Across three MLLM base models, DAPO-V improves overall accuracy while substantially improving accuracy on elusive hallucination questions by 5.78--9.72 percentage points over accuracy-only DAPO. We further test DAPO-V with a blinded human study, which shows that hallucinations are more readily detected in DAPO-V than those of the base model.

Abstract: Episodic-memory embodied question answering enables agents to answer questions based on previously recorded visual observations of the surrounding scenes. However, growing observation histories, varying observation quality across viewpoints, and limited computational resources make it challenging to construct and maintain compact visual memories while retrieving sufficient evidence for question answering. To address these challenges, we introduce CoverMem, which incorporates three key components: (1) a coverage-constrained memory construction method that selects a compact set of informative full frames satisfying object-quality and representative-scene coverage requirements; (2) a coverage-preserving incremental maintenance strategy that locally reselects only the affected frames to satisfy updated coverage requirements as observations arrive; and (3) a budgeted complementary frame selection method that balances question relevance, additional support for question requirements, and redundancy with selected frames within a fixed budget. Experimental results on three benchmarks covering diverse environments and question types demonstrate that CoverMem improves question-answering performance under limited visual budgets.

Abstract: Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to relate past experience, current spatial state, and future motion while following natural-language instructions. Recent approaches improve these capabilities through long-horizon memory, state-aware reasoning, and future prediction, yet these components are often modeled separately, leaving navigation state evolution insufficiently connected across time. We argue that camera pose provides a natural geometric representation for bridging past, present, and future states. Based on this insight, we propose PoseVLN, a pose-aware VLN framework that jointly learns navigation decisions and geometric state evolution. PoseVLN organizes visual memory and the current observation in an episode-relative coordinate frame, and jointly predicts the next K navigation actions together with a local $Δ$Pose over the same horizon. The predicted $Δ$Pose is composed with the current trajectory-level pose to obtain the future pose state, which is recursively propagated throughout navigation, forming a continuous geometric state evolution from past memory to future motion. Experiments on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE Val-Unseen splits demonstrate the effectiveness of explicitly modeling geometric state evolution, with PoseVLN outperforming the strongest compared baselines by 2.6 and 2.9 percentage points in success rate, respectively, using only single RGB observations.

Experiments on STEM and general visual reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across model scales over reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation baselines. Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models advance robotic manipulation, but their reliance on demonstrations exposes them to backdoor threats. Among these threats, trajectory-level targeted backdoors are particularly challenging and may pose greater safety risks than behavior-disrupting attacks: rather than merely causing task failures or local action deviations, they must sustain closed-loop redirection to complete an attacker-specified multi-step task. To reliably induce such behaviors, existing methods typically rely on high poisoning rates to obtain sufficient target-trajectory supervision. However, high poisoning rates may compromise clean-task performance, whereas low rates leave target-trajectory supervision scarce and backdoor learning vulnerable to interference from dominant clean-task updates. To resolve this trade-off at low poisoning rates, we propose a two-stage co-optimization framework guided by vision, language, and action. Stage I freezes the policy and uses differentiable rendering and multimodal supervision to optimize a 3D trigger's texture, translation, and scale, strengthening trigger-target associations from limited poisoned demonstrations. Building on the optimized trigger, Stage II applies source-selective dual-LoRA adaptation to decouple clean and backdoor updates and mitigate their interference, followed by learned column-wise calibration and analytic fusion into a unified policy. Experiments on OpenVLA, OpenVLA-OFT, $π_0$, and $π_{0.5}$ across four LIBERO suites show consistent gains over all evaluated attack baselines. With only 1% poisoned data, our method achieves 86.4% target success and 95.2% clean-task success on OpenVLA-OFT in LIBERO-Spatial. Real-robot trials also demonstrate trajectory-level hijacking.

</details>

Abstract: Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. Training supervision is then derived from candidate-expert geometric agreement and independently recomputed feasibility metrics. At inference, the reasoner uses patient evidence and surgical requirements to select or revise an action, which an independent geometry engine subsequently materializes and verifies. GeoSurge achieves the best overall scores on DeepTumour Liver and CRLM-QA, improving the constraint satisfaction rate from 73% to 100% on CRLM and from 56% to 99% on GeoSurge-Bench.

Abstract: Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? To answer this question, we define an economic world model (EWM) as an agent's internal representation of how an economic system works, enabling counterfactual simulation and decision-making under resource constraints. We then propose a general framework for studying EWMs that links three tasks: simulating agent behaviour, learning economic transitions and systematically evaluating the dynamics. As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. Together, we aim not only to reproduce real economic dynamics, but also to discover and test new mechanisms that support better decisions and benefit society.

</details>

Abstract: In Minecraft, a common testbed for embodied agents, semantic tools such as mineBlock pick targets from the simulator's loaded world state without a visibility test, so a buried ore the agent has never seen is one call away, and scores cannot show whether the agent searched. We formalize the missing requirement as evidence closure: every decision of an agent system, including those inside its tools, may depend on the world only through facts for which the agent holds evidence. EviCraft is a runtime and benchmark that enforces closure below the agent and makes the admission rule an experimental variable: a perception-grounded regime (G) admits only what is in line of sight or returned by the agent's own actions, and a loaded-state regime (L) reproduces standard tool stacks, with agents, tools, and worlds held fixed. On Survival, the regime effect exceeds the strongest agent's lead: under G, three mid-sized agents reach the diamond in 32%-40% of runs, against 76%-94% under L, and the strongest agent under G scores below each of them under L. In two models, removing only the line-of-sight test closes 93%-96% of the score gap, and loading state only inside tools recovers 70%-98%, so safeguards on observations and queries leave most of the leak open. In open-ended acquisition, the gain from L concentrates in items that depend on buried resources or mobs. Code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/AllenReder/EviCraft

Abstract: Latent world models enable goal-directed planning from offline visual data, but prediction alone does not specify which action-related distinctions their representations should preserve. Inverse dynamics provides auxiliary supervision, yet squared-error action regression rewards additional information only through changes in the conditional action mean. We propose Contrastive Identification of Actions (CIA), which identifies the executed action among alternatives sampled from a history-conditioned behavior proposal. Comparing actions plausible in the same context targets information supplied by the next latent beyond history. Abstract: Computers and their interfaces are usually treated as fixed, so a person, or increasingly an AI agent, must adapt to whatever the operating system presents. This is limiting when someone needs a different workflow, or an interface suited to hands-free use or a disability. We explore an overlooked alternative. Rather than training an agent to operate an existing computer, we train a generative model to be the computer, predicting each next screen from past screens and the user's mouse and keyboard actions. Treating a desktop as an interactive world model raises problems that natural-video world models largely avoid. Without a camera pose to index memory, the model must remember content that has left the screen without any geometric cues. Arithmetic in a terminal has to be exactly right, not merely plausible. Small interface text must also stay legible at interactive frame rates. Our Interactive Neural Computer is structured like a Neural Turing Machine and starts to address these problems with three modules trained end to end from pixels and actions. A surprise-gated slot memory, supervised by latent next-frame prediction (MEMORY-JEPA), stores what is off screen. An exploratory query bridge brings in the reasoning of a pretrained VLM, and a residual renderer predicts only the latent changes between frames. In long closed-loop rollouts, our model recalls occluded files in 64.2% of trials (chance is 33.3%), compared with at most 56.0% for recurrent and sliding-window baselines. It also raises terminal-text OCR accuracy from 1.3% to 50.1%. Finally, because the memory is explicit, it can be transplanted at test time to causally change what the model renders.

</details>

Abstract: AI agents working on long tasks often commit to an answer before they have enough evidence, a behavior called laziness. In our evaluations, they frequently commit even while their stated belief still names several possible answers. An outcome reward at the end of the episode scores only the final answer, so it cannot teach the agent when to commit, because a lucky guess receives the same reward as an answer backed by evidence. During training, however, the simulator can compute the exact posterior over the answer at every step and tell whether the evidence is already enough. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Beliefs (RLVB), which rewards the agent for taking further steps while this posterior still allows several answers and for committing once the evidence is sufficient. The reward needs no judge, critic or reward model, and it never reads the belief line the agent writes before each action. We also introduce Pro Worlds, a benchmark of visual puzzles where the agent views a large world through a small window. We evaluate three vision-language models on three benchmarks, two of which are never seen in training. RLVB improves the base model in all nine settings, by 17.1% on average. Under the outcome reward, GRPO and PPO fall below the base model on average. RLVB also outperforms PPO with a process reward from an LLM judge by 9.3% on average while using 1.6 times fewer GPU-hours. The trained agent answers with sufficient evidence in 51%, 12% and 74% of the episodes on the three benchmarks, against 8%, 0.4% and 36% for the base model. Its accuracy gain therefore comes from answering more often at high levels of evidence. The belief line shows how the agent reaches this evidence: it acts on its own belief. It commits at only 0.1 to 2.2% of the turns at which the evidence is insufficient and its line still names several answers, against 3 to 68% for the base model.

</details>

Abstract: Systematic conservation planning must allocate limited protection budgets sequentially under ecological and climatic uncertainty. Static tools cannot adapt as conditions change, and policies optimised only for expected outcomes can fail on the worst-case trajectories climate change makes increasingly likely. CAPTAIN was the first framework to cast this as sequential reinforcement learning, but its gradient-free Evolution Strategies (ES) optimiser lacks per-timestep credit assignment. We replace ES with Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO) via a Plackett-Luce solution to CAPTAIN's non-differentiable action space, and replace its scalar critic with an Implicit Quantile Network (IQN) modelling the full return distribution. A matched-granularity comparison shows decision granularity, not the optimiser, drives most of the ecological gain, so the real contribution is training efficiency and extensibility to risk-sensitive objectives. On a New Zealand marine dataset, it matches or exceeds ES's outcomes at a fraction of the training cost, offering a template for future work ES's black-box design could not support

Abstract: AI-native radio access network (RAN) control involves interdependent decisions across network entities, protocol layers, and timescales. Understanding how AI and conventional methods can jointly support these decisions requires a common basis for comparing their functional roles. This survey introduces a technique-agnostic taxonomy of RAN decision-making organized around four complementary capabilities: Representation and Reasoning, Prediction and Simulation, Adaptive Control, and Decision Synthesis. These capabilities are defined by their roles in the control process, independently of the techniques used to implement them. Through this perspective, we examine representative AI and model-based approaches, including foundation models, world models, reinforcement learning, and optimization. We then apply the taxonomy to representative RAN control solutions across slow, medium, fast, and cross-timescale applications to identify which capabilities they implement and how these capabilities are combined in hybrid AI systems. We also review datasets, benchmarks, simulators, digital twins, and experimental platforms according to their support for the decision-making process and reproducible evaluation. The review highlights a gap between the availability of resources for closed-loop experimentation and the standardization of tasks, metrics, and evaluation protocols needed for meaningful comparisons.Finally, we identify research directions for hybrid AI in AI-native RAN, including capability integration, multi-agent and cross-timescale coordination, constraint handling, and reproducible closed-loop evaluation. A curated list of surveyed works and evaluation resources is available at https://github.com/pegahani/Hybrid-AI-RAN-Survey.

Abstract: Intermediate layers often outperform final layers when foundation models are used as frozen feature extractors, but the source of this advantage is unclear. We study this question in EEG foundation models by separating three factors: how representations are read out, whether intermediate layers provide predictive utility beyond the final-layer interface, and whether useful depth structure transfers across datasets. Across five models and three tasks, improving the final-layer readout consistently recovers substantial predictive performance, yet intermediate layers remain advantageous within frozen extraction. We also find that layers selected on one dataset remain useful on another across five dataset pairs and four task families, and that a compact set of source-derived task directions retains predictive value after transfer in sleep staging. Together, these results show that predictive utility varies across depth, and its usefulness depends on how representations are read out and transferred. More crucially, source-selected layers provide a reusable feature interface across datasets, reducing the need for target-side layer search while outperforming final-layer baselines on target datasets.

Abstract: Algorithmic content moderation is increasingly important for governing short-form video content, yet harmful-content detection research focuses primarily on prediction or limited explanations. This leaves a governance challenge underexplored: producing decision-linked evidence that supports the review and justification of moderation decisions. Using toxic diet culture as an empirical case, we conceptualize governance-ready evidence as computational traceability, evidence localization, policy grounding, and evidence inspectability. We develop the Sparse Autoencoder-based Policy-Aligned Contrastive Evidence (SPACE) framework in a pretrained SAE feature space derived from a large multimodal model. A global relevance gate and decomposable linear evidence head identify decision-linked evidence; segment supervision and within-video ranking localize this evidence; policy alignment maps it to platform guidelines; and Pareto-efficient selection yields a compact, task-specific evidence vocabulary. On 3,711 labeled TikTok videos, SPACE achieves the highest harmful-category Macro F1 among benchmarks across four families, at 0.8507. Its highest-scoring observation overlaps a ground-truth harmful span for 88.0% of harmful test videos, while its evidence representation retrieves the corresponding policy category for 87.0%. Stakeholder evaluations indicate that the SPACE evidence report is perceived as useful for understanding, reviewing, documenting, and responding to moderation decisions. The study contributes computational design science knowledge for embedding governance-ready evidence within predictive content-moderation systems.

Abstract: World models is answering ``imagine" answers to ``what-if'' questions via imagined rollouts. But rollouts collected by adaptive policies induce treatment-outcome confounding in imagined rollouts. We study this problem in Match-3 difficulty adjustment, where latent player skill influences both assigned difficulty and churn. We build a fully specified Match-3 SCM with sequential mechanics, adaptive assignment, and paired ground-truth interventions, on which unadjusted models fit to the logged trajectories select difficulty settings that raise churn. We then introduce Causal Latent Adjustment (CaLA), a DAG-constrained world model that infers a variational player representation from pretreatment history. Across our evaluations, CaLA provides the most reliable population-level intervention estimates and competitive individualized decisions, although rich handcrafted histories can remain advantageous for individual-level adjustment. We further find that residual correction can degrade causal decision quality despite improving predictive fit, highlighting the need to distinguish predictive accuracy, confounding adjustment and the behavior of downstream causal estimators.

Representational analysis shows that drift concentrates in the multimodal and action features. Linear probes indicate that OOD features still retain task-relevant information, but appear unreadable, consistent with representation misalignment. These findings suggest that even when ID stability remains intact, severe OOD forgetting can still occur, accompanied by representational drift during continual learning.

Abstract: Foundation Models (FMs) are envisioned to become an integral component of intelligent federated edge networks because of their strong representation and generalization capabilities. However, their integration into resource-constrained and privacy-sensitive edge environments remains challenging. This challenge is particularly pronounced in data-free settings, where no shared, public, or server-accessible reference dataset is available to establish a common reference space for aligning distributed knowledge. To address this challenge, we propose ECLIPSE, which is an embedding-centered FM-based federated knowledge transfer framework developed for data-free edge intelligence. ECLIPSE enables edge intelligence capitalizing on a server-hosted FM without sharing raw data, local models, or a public reference dataset. It establishes a shared embedding reference space through which edge devices encode their local task knowledge. Only the resulting embeddings are transmitted to the server for alignment and aggregation. Using the aggregated embeddings, the server constructs a task-aware surrogate dataset through an embedding-guided generative process. This surrogate dataset is then used to adapt the server-hosted FM to the downstream task. The adapted FM is subsequently distilled into a lightweight global model and distributed to the edge devices. This process allows edge devices to benefit from both distributed task knowledge and the generalization capabilities of the FM. Experimental results across diverse classification datasets show that ECLIPSE outperforms conventional Federated Learning (FL) and data-free Federated Knowledge-Transfer (FKT) baselines. It also achieves competitive performance compared with databased approaches. Furthermore, the framework remains robust under statistical and model heterogeneity across different data distributions and local architectures.

</details>

Abstract: Sim-to-real reinforcement learning (RL) has enabled impressive robotic behaviors, yet deploying policies directly from vision remains challenging. A common approach first trains a teacher policy with RL using privileged state observations, then distills its behavior into a vision-based student via imitation. However, the resulting student can substantially underperform its teacher. Directly training visual policies with RL offers a promising alternative, but learning task-relevant visual representations from scratch can be sample-inefficient, making one-phase visual RL intractable. We propose DRIFT, a simple recipe for efficient one-phase visual RL using image observations available at test time. Our key insight is to use frozen, pretrained foundation vision encoders, allowing RL to focus on learning control from existing visual representations. Freezing the encoder avoids costly backpropagation through the vision backbone, while its pretrained representations enable real-world transfer from fast, rasterized simulation rendering. Under matched architectures, observations, and compute budgets, DRIFT outperforms two-phase distillation and achieves a 58.8% relative improvement in average real-world success rate across three manipulation tasks. Controlled simulation evaluations demonstrate that DRIFT's relative advantage grows from roughly 9% in-distribution to over 61% under out-of-distribution shifts, suggesting that stronger generalization contributes to superior real-world transfer. Abstract: A foundation model for time series is pretrained once and then asked to forecast series it has never seen, so the evidence for it is its score on held-out datasets. Such datasets are scarce, and a few public leaderboards carry most comparisons, yet none of them states how small a difference its datasets can detect. We call that quantity the resolution of a board and introduce Vernier, a tool that computes it from the files a board already publishes. Vernier also reports which entries a board cannot separate from its leader and whether its order changes when unrelated parties submit. GIFT-Eval resolves 6.8 rank places, and its displayed neighbours sit 0.91 apart. The ten places of its top ten hold fewer than two resolvable steps. fev-bench, Chronos Benchmark II and TIME show the same pattern: their resolution is 3.6 to 19 times the gap between neighbours. With each of its 2,554 series as an independent source, BOOM is the one board that resolves its top ten. Below the resolution the order depends on who else submitted: across four boards, rules that rank against the pool reversed 43 top-ten pairs with neither entry rerun, in one update in seven. Boards also do not record how much history a model receives. On 70 configurations, shortening it raised the error of three of four open models, two of them by 15%. We release Vernier, which runs this report with one command on any leaderboard that publishes per-dataset scores: https://github.com/therealram18/vernier

Abstract: Gradient-based actor optimization is central to deep reinforcement learning. However, conventional first-order optimizers do not explicitly exploit the local curvature of neural actor losses. We propose EQUIP (Curvature-Equilibrated Gradients for Policy Search), a novel approach that seeks to balance local contraction and expansion of the actor parameter space by equilibrating positive and negative curvature. EQUIP constructs a trajectory-informed search subspace and uses the projected Hessian of actor loss to build a bounded local metric for curvature equilibration. First-order loss matching preserves the approximated first-order decrease in actor loss. Meanwhile, gradient mixing and curvature-based gradient selection adjust the gradient applied for actor optimization. We establish the geometric properties of curvature equilibration, derive bounds on gradient alignment and magnitude, and identify conditions for improved actor loss reduction and convergence toward stationarity under direct gradient updates. We integrate EQUIP into Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) without modifying its actor loss, critic learning, or replay mechanism. Experiments on 6 MuJoCo continuous control benchmarks show that the resulting EQUIP-SAC algorithm achieves higher mean sample efficiency than SAC and two second-order policy optimization baselines. Ablation studies further highlight the benefits of curvature equilibration and trajectory-informed geometry for policy training.

Under patch tokenization (PatchTST), the FFN's gain exceeds attention's in probing on all 8 datasets, and its 8-dataset mean is 3.2x attention's. Without attention, the FFN alone reaches nearly the same total gain, both when attention is removed from a trained model and when PatchTST is trained without it, and the retrained model forecasts within a few percent of the full model. In a controlled manipulation of PatchTST, single-scalar tokens (P=1) nearly eliminate the FFN's construction, while one more point per token (P=2) restores it. Our forecasting-side interventions (activation patching, knockout, and training without attention) establish component-level reliance and sufficiency. These results offer a representational reading of prior findings on the gains from patching, the competitiveness of self-attention-free models (TSMixer, PatchMLP), and the distinct role of cross-attention in CATS.

Abstract: Large-scale pretraining provides embodied models with general capabilities, but reliable task execution still requires post training. In multitask settings, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) faces distribution mismatch between training and deployment, whereas reinforcement learning (RL) is limited by sparse rewards and uneven learning progress across tasks. Multi-teacher on policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates expert capabilities through dense supervision on the model's own trajectories, but achieving performance beyond the teachers remains challenging. Stage 1 efficiently consolidates expert capabilities through MOPD. Experiments across multiple models and benchmarks show that the framework outperforms joint SFT and exceeds the corresponding teachers' aggregate success rates. Abstract: Remaining useful life (RUL) prediction is central to Prognostics and Health Management (PHM). The growth of industrial IoT has unlocked large multivariate sensor streams and enabled deep models - especially Transformers - for long-horizon sequence learning. However, transformers are parameter-heavy and data-hungry, while operational datasets are often siloed and privacy-sensitive. Federated Learning (FL) addresses this by training across clients without raw data sharing, but in practice many clients connect over lossy wireless links where UDP is preferred for low latency, making model exchange vulnerable to packet drops. This framework is trained with QMIX value-function factorization for scalability. We instantiate the approach with a Graph Convolution Position-Aware Transformer (GCPATr) for RUL forecasting. On the CMAPSS dataset, our MARL-enhanced FL (RL-FL) consistently improves mean square error over standard FL, with the largest gains at high packet loss (e.g., up to 18% improvement on FD003 at 50% loss). These results show that MARL is an effective countermeasure to wireless degradation in FL-based RUL prediction.

Abstract: Latent world models learn representations by predicting future states in an abstract embedding space rather than reconstructing raw pixels. When training across multi-frame sequences, however, not all frames and spatial regions are equally informative or predictable. We argue that forcing a world model to minimize every prediction error may be unnecessary and harmful. Under standard $L_2$ loss, unbounded gradients force the encoder and predictor to overfit noisy or transient frames at the expense of core physical structure, while amplifying gradient conflict with representation regularizers. Conversely, $L_1$ loss bounds large gradients, but its constant gradient magnitude near zero disrupts the local smoothness required for planning. We resolve this tension with \textbf{ReJEPA}, a bounded predictive regulation principle that combines bounded gradients for large errors with smooth quadratic behavior near zero, instantiated simply via the element-wise $\log\cosh$ loss.
  As a drop-in training replacement across Temporal Straightening, LeWorldModel, and V-JEPA~2.1, $\log\cosh$ consistently outperforms $L_2$, a curvature-matched quadratic control, $L_1$, and Smooth-$L_1$ baselines without altering architectures or inference procedures. Notably, the gains grow with environmental complexity, scaling from simple navigation to contact-rich manipulation and real-world autonomous driving on NAVSIM. Probing and geometric analyses confirm that relaxing the penalty on extreme errors yields representations that better capture physical states and align with the true planning cost landscape.

</details>

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have attracted increasing attention in cross-domain forecasting. Existing works mainly rely on large-scale pre-training and general modeling paradigms. However, the former heavily relies on the diversity and domain coverage of pre-training data. Meanwhile, the latter learns unified representations across heterogeneous domains without explicitly identifying the temporal regularities shared among them, limiting robust cross-domain generalization. This work explores cross-domain generalization by characterizing commonalities in recurring local dynamics across domains, rather than relying solely on implicitly learned temporal representations. To capture such cross-domain commonalities, we propose Time Series Foundation Units (TFUs) as reusable building blocks discovered from multi-scale temporal segments, each characterizing a recurring local dynamic pattern shared across domains. Experiments on seven public datasets show that UniTFU substantially outperforms existing TSFMs in cross-domain forecasting and can efficiently adapt to target-domain dynamics by updating its reusable components with limited target data. Abstract: Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop can be read out through the shared language-model head. Post-training them with verifiable rewards is hard: the outcome reward carries one bit per response, spreading it over loops trains intermediate loops on tokens sampled from the last loop, and distillation needs either a teacher that ignores the reward or an external, larger model. We observe that a looped model already contains a teacher stronger than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELGO (Extrapolated-Loop Group-relative Optimization) adds to GRPO a dense per-token distillation term towards this distribution. The teacher is computed from an anchor copy of the model, which stays frozen and is replaced by the current model once the student's KL to the teacher falls below a threshold, so the teacher improves with the student without chasing its own extrapolation. The outcome reward splits probability between correct and incorrect responses, and the teacher decides which tokens are preferred inside a response. Between refreshes the objective is KL-regularised RL anchored at the teacher, whose optimum tilts the teacher by the reward. On Ouro-1.4B-Thinking, ELGO raises the average avg@8 over five math benchmarks from 62.32 with GRPO to 64.20 and is the best compared method on every benchmark, while spreading the reward over loops falls below the base model.

</details>

Abstract: Latent world models learn compact recurrent dynamics from offline trajectories, yet deployed simulators can retain backdoor triggers or stale transitions from outdated physical regimes. Existing unlearning methods fail in autoregressive physics: gradient updates distort latent manifolds and compound rollout drift, naive output zeroing suppresses valid control responses on trigger onset, and single-step deletion leaves corruption inside recurrent hidden states. We propose Analytical Dynamics Excision (ADE), a deterministic, zero-training closed-form unlearning algorithm for latent world models. Using only cached pretraining moments, ADE recovers retain covariances via single-pass matrix downdating without raw retain data and cascades closed-form edits across the input bottleneck, hidden states, and downstream heads. Pairing orthogonal excision with whitened recurrent damping and mean-preserving affine calibration neutralizes out-of-manifold backdoors and realigns in-manifold shifts without variance collapse.
