Abstract: As large language models tackle longer and more complex tasks, understanding when a critic improves the efficiency of reinforcement learning (RL) becomes increasingly important. Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) trains a critic to reduce variance and provide fine-grained credit assignment, while Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) drops the critic and avoids the bias of bootstrapping from the critic's value estimates. Although recent critic-based methods have reported strong performance relative to GRPO, it remains unclear when each approach is preferable. We argue that there is no universal answer; instead, the choice depends on several interacting factors, including batch size, critic reliance, and task complexity. We study the bias-variance tradeoff in terms of gradient direction, measuring how closely each method's gradient estimates align with the true policy gradient as batch size grows. Under a shared actor objective, we compare GRPO with PPO in control tasks, short-horizon math, and long-horizon terminal agents. Across more than 1,200 training runs, including 96 with LLMs, we find that PPO generally leads at small batch sizes, while GRPO often overtakes heavily bootstrapped PPO and reaches parity with lightly bootstrapped PPO at larger batches. At longer horizons and in more complex domains, GRPO needs larger batches to catch up to PPO's performance. Accounting for training compute, critics pay off at small budgets, but their returns diminish at larger budgets.

</details>

Abstract: Programmatic verifiers provide scalable reward signals for training coding agents with reinforcement learning, yet subtle harness defects and incomplete unit tests allow policies to earn full credit without solving the intended task. Existing training-time defenses intervene either by penalizing suspicious outputs or by filtering flagged tasks and rollouts. However, output penalties leave the underlying verifier exploitable, driving policies to disguise shortcuts under reasoning monitors or zeroing out valid solutions under detector false positives, whereas task and rollout filtering discards or mutes the hardest problems where shortcuts emerge earliest. Pre-training verifier hardening repairs the environment directly, but misses vulnerability classes that surface only as the policy improves during reinforcement learning. In this paper, we propose online environment repair, which leaves triggering rollout rewards unchanged to remove hiding incentives and asynchronously patches exploited environments during training. Candidate repairs triggered by execution invariants or differential fuzzing are deployed only after passing three automated validation gates that block the exploit while preserving valid solutions, canceling detector false positives and enabling structural propagation across repositories without scaffold labels or reference solutions. Across 20 controlled GRPO experiments on heterogeneous software-engineering repositories, online environment repair reduces cumulative reward hacking from 36.9% to 0.06% and improves held-out Pass@8 from 21.7% to 46.7%, consistently outperforming reward-penalty, rollout-filtering, and static-hardening baselines while eliminating zero-shot exploitation on unseen loophole classes.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models have shown strong capabilities on complex tasks, yet how to control their reasoning effort remains a significant challenge for practical deployment. This paper provides a systematic investigation of reasoning-effort training, contrasting reinforcement learning (RL) with on-policy distillation (OPD). We find that compressing reasoning and separating effort tiers are governed by different signals. Length-penalized RL reliably compresses a single policy, reducing thinking on AIME25 by 70\% without accuracy loss, but collapses the tiers when they are trained jointly. Multi-teacher OPD separates them: across 33 benchmarks, low and medium use 58\% and 43\% fewer thinking tokens than high, while high improves on the reference policy by 3.1 points. We trace this difference to the training signal. A sequence-level reward cannot tell tier-specific behavior from shared behavior, whereas OPD supplies a tier-specific target at every visited state, including the decision to stop. Building on this analysis, we show that a single token-level KL anchor on the high tier is sufficient to learn separated tiers within one RL stage, and that natural-language effort instructions make this separation emerge from the start of training, yielding a policy competitive with multi-teacher OPD at matched thinking length. Finally, the separation built by dense targets holds only on the states seen in training: it does not carry over to unseen agentic tasks, where effort acts mainly through the number of interaction turns, whereas the compression induced by sequence-level rewards does.

Abstract: A learned critic can improve a pretrained flow-matching policy by steering sampled actions toward higher predicted value. However, even a critic that fits the behavior data well can provide misleading action gradients. Gradient components pointing away from the data-supported region are weakly constrained by behavior data and can lead guidance to exploit critic extrapolation errors. A single velocity evaluation of the frozen flow defines a denoising map. RetraQ filters the critic gradient with the map's Jacobian and applies the map itself to correct the updated action. The same update guides actions at test time and provides targets for policy distillation and online reinforcement learning. In controlled studies, RetraQ improves true return while keeping refined actions close to the data-supported region, and its distillation targets remain more stable than those of raw critic ascent. In offline-to-online training on LIBERO and OGBench, RetraQ achieves higher mean final success than the evaluated critic-guidance baselines. On a real-world plug-insertion task, test-time RetraQ improves the success rate of a behavior-cloned $π_{0.5}$ policy from 55.0% to 76.7%. Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a powerful approach for improving language-model reasoning, but its supervision is inherently sparse: a sequence-level verifier reward is propagated only through sampled trajectories and provides no direct signal for alternative actions. On-policy distillation (OPD) offers a complementary form of supervision by providing dense teacher feedback over the full next-token distribution, but this signal is not grounded in verified task outcomes. Simply co-optimizing the two objectives does not couple these signals: verifier feedback remains sparse and does not inform the dense teacher supervision. We introduce Verifiable On-Policy Distillation (VOPD), which transfers sparse verifier feedback into dense, verifier-grounded supervision. Our key observation is that the implicit-reward view of OPD places teacher supervision and verifier feedback in a common reward space while retaining a reward signal available for every next-token action. Under this view, VOPD projects verifier preference onto the teacher's implicit-reward direction and Rao--Blackwellizes the resulting signal over the full vocabulary, propagating verifier-grounded supervision to actions that were never sampled. The resulting objective can also be interpreted as adaptively extrapolating the distillation target according to teacher--verifier agreement. Across two teacher--student configurations and eight mathematical reasoning benchmarks, VOPD consistently improves both final performance and learning efficiency over vanilla OPD and other on-policy distillation baselines. Our code is available at https://github.com/juntongshi48/VOPD.

</details>

Abstract: Many applications of machine learning on tabular data need both a classifier and a generator of realistic records. The default is to train two separate models, whereas a single joint model promises both from one set of weights. Several families of tabular deep learning models can be turned into such a joint model, which raises the question of which joint model is preferable at which computational budget. In this empirical study, we introduce and compare joint models based on four state-of-the-art tabular data modeling families (energy-based models, probabilistic circuits, tabular foundation models and tabular diffusion models) against measured FLOPs and wall-clock time, on 29 classification datasets. Methods are evaluated based on two defined aggregate scores that take into account different aspects of prediction and generation. We then also compare these joint models with every pair of a separate classifier and generator at different computational budgets, to see when a joint model may be a defendible choice against the regular practice of keeping a separate classifier and generator. We find that the best joint model is a probabilistic circuit at the smallest budgets and, beyond that, a tabular foundation model that generates column by column using a regressor (TabICL). Compared with pairs of separate models, this study finds a joint model is rarely preferable but often very close to the best pair, without the hassle of training and maintaining two separate models.

Abstract: Multimodal protein language models unify sequence and discrete structural tokens via iterative masked decoding, offering a promising paradigm for joint protein design. However, standard decoders rely on a memoryless mask reset at each iteration: only a sparse subset of newly committed hard tokens is carried forward, while all remaining unresolved sites are reset to an identical [MASK] embedding. In open-ended co-generation where neither modality provides a fixed external anchor, this stateless transition limits single-step refinement depth and deprives both tracks of stable cross-step soft anchors, leading to slow convergence or degenerative repetitive collapse. Observing that uncommitted predictions evolve with pronounced temporal coherence rather than erratic noise, we introduce Predictive Memory (PredMem), a training-free self-conditioning recurrence that carries these soft beliefs across steps. At each step, PredMem summarizes raw logits into a probability-weighted Top-$k$ expectation embedding at every residue and affinely interpolates it into eligible masked inputs on the next step at zero extra denoiser cost. Across DPLM-2, DPLM-2.1, and ESM3, PredMem raises unconditional sequence-structure co-generation success by 3.6, 17.7, and 48.7 percentage points, respectively, while improving the quality-diversity trade-off and suppressing repetitive mode collapse. Controlled diagnostics show that these gains stem primarily from early trajectory stabilization in unanchored cross-modal co-evolution rather than extra compute or simple entropy reduction, establishing PredMem as an efficient inference mechanism for multimodal discrete generation.

</details>

Abstract: Visual world models enable goal-directed planning by predicting action outcomes. Gradient-based planners optimize actions to lower predicted goal cost, but continued optimization can exploit prediction errors after execution stops improving. Comparing plans requires informative relative scores; improving them also requires reliable update directions. Exact-state execution reveals that a frozen hierarchical world model ranks goal-conditioned proposals even when its rankings among outputs of prolonged gradient search are uninformative. Effective planning pairs goal-directed candidates with predictions that distinguish their outcomes. We introduce CriticJEPA, which learns goal-conditioned flow proposals from reward-free trajectories and uses frozen rollouts to select among them at every hierarchy level. Forward selection forms a complete planner; short anchored updates refine candidates before selection. Across visual navigation and manipulation, CriticJEPA increases success at a fraction of the planning compute, transferring shared settings across independently trained models, hierarchy depths, and unseen tasks while keeping every world-model weight fixed.

</details>

Abstract: We address the problem of learning audio-visual representations from co-occurring modalities. Multimodal contrastive learning typically assumes that paired observations provide shared views of the same underlying information. While this may be true for datasets collected in constrained environments, it does not always hold. For instance, co-occurring audio and visual signals can differ substantially in their content due to off-screen sounds. Nevertheless, standard contrastive objectives treat all paired samples as equally aligned. In this work, we propose Caption Guided Alignment Aware Loss (CGAAL), a model-agnostic objective that leverages modality-specific captions to estimate semantic agreement between audio and video. CGAAL uses these agreement scores to weight the contribution of each positive pair. A warm-up period and gradual thresholding strategy progressively introduce this alignment-aware training signal. We demonstrate the generality of CGAAL by applying it to three contrastive baselines, consistently improving their performance on audio-visual retrieval.

Abstract: Universal Multimodal Embedding (UME) models map heterogeneous multimodal inputs into a shared embedding space, where pairwise similarity supports diverse downstream tasks. Recent reasoning-driven UME approaches use intermediate reasoning to generate additional context for embedding, but often incur extra computation at inference. In this paper, we explore the matched candidate (i.e., the paired target) as an alternative source of context, asking whether a model can "see the answer" during training and "learn to embed" without it at inference. However, direct conditioning on this privileged context can introduce candidate-specific information unavailable from the query alone or cause the candidate-conditioned query representation to collapse toward the candidate embedding. To address this, we introduce Hindsight-Enhanced Multimodal Embeddings (HEME), which learns query-grounded hindsight representations from such candidate context. HEME first trains a hindsight teacher with a hindsight attention mask, mismatched-candidate conditioning, and query-token alignment to limit excessive reliance on the candidate. It then freezes the teacher and distills its hindsight representation and similarity distribution into a standalone student embedder. At inference, the student independently encodes queries and candidates without candidate conditioning or the hindsight teacher. Experiments on MMEB-V2 and the agent and text tasks of MMEB-V3 demonstrate the effectiveness of HEME and show that it enables more efficient inference than explicit reasoning-driven methods.

</details>

Abstract: Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop is readable through the shared language-model head. Post-training them is hard. Reward-only reinforcement learning needs a verifier and learns from one sparse signal per response; spreading the reward over loops collapses the entropy of every loop; and cross-loop self-distillation, as in LoopOPD, can only teach intermediate loops, because the last loop cannot learn from its own frozen copy: the loss is zero at initialisation. We observe that a looped model already contains a policy that decodes better than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELPSD (Extrapolated-Loop Policy Self-Distillation) turns this distribution into a self-distillation target: a frozen copy of the model computes it, and the model's own last loop is distilled towards it on its own rollouts, using the exact reverse KL at every visited prefix. Teacher and student are the same model, so ELPSD needs no reward, no external teacher and no privileged context, and it leaves rollouts and decoding unchanged. We show that the objective pulls the last loop towards the base model's last loop with weight 1+w and away from the base model's first loop with weight w, and that the teacher must stay frozen, since an idealised teacher recomputed from the current model extrapolates without bound. We evaluate ELPSD on Ouro-1.4B-Thinking across five math benchmarks against the base model, GRPO, RLTT and OPSD. Without any reward, ELPSD raises the average avg@8 from 61.15 to 63.69 and is the best method on all five benchmarks, ahead of GRPO (62.32), which uses a verifier, and the self-distillation method OPSD (61.77), which needs reference solutions.

</details>

Abstract: As the use of reinforcement learning (RL) becomes more widespread in cyber-physical systems, it becomes increasingly important to design RL agents that are able to adapt to changes in their environments in order to maintain safety and reliability. This paper addresses the question of whether different classes of RL algorithm are more robust against forgetting previously learned behaviours, a critical issue in continual learning, in non-stationary environments. We evaluate the robustness of representative examples of three important classes of RL algorithm in a non-stationary autonomous parking environment. These are Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which is a model-free on-policy algorithm, Soft-Actor-Critic (SAC), which is a model-free off-policy algorithm, and Drama, which is a model-based algorithm. The agents must learn to successfully park in four different scenarios, which are presented successively, with parking spaces oriented at varying angles. We find off-policy algorithms appear to be more robust compared to on-policy algorithms due to their use of replay, while model-based algorithms do not appear to offer any direct advantage over model-free algorithms. These experiments also highlight the additional challenges of finding robust abstractions of the environment, and oversensitivity to hyperparameters.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have emerged as a serious competitor to gradient-boosted trees, predicting on a new table in a single forward pass by in-context learning. However, their attention is quadratic in the number of samples and features, which caps pretraining at small synthetic tables and forces inference on large ones to rely on chunking or subsampling. Row-compression models reduce the feature-wise cost, but only by discarding the cell-level representation, and remain quadratic in the number of samples. We introduce InducedTFM, which replaces both attention stages of a bidimensional TFM with the induced attention of the Set Transformer, preserving cell-level representations while making its cost linear in both samples and features. Under a training budget matched to a full-attention TabICLv2 reimplementation, InducedTFM matches its accuracy on TabArena and on a benchmark of larger datasets, and clearly outperforms a kernel linear-attention baseline of the same asymptotic cost. It overtakes TabICLv2 in inference time on narrow tables, reaching a 30x speed-up at 640K rows, although row compression remains preferable on wide tables.

Abstract: Real-world generative problems in biology, medical imaging, or robotics, rarely come with perfectly paired source and target distributions. While unbalanced Optimal Transport (uOT) provides a robust framework to bridge unpaired datasets via minimum-length paths in probability space, existing formulations based on the Wasserstein-Fisher-Rao (WFR) geometry introduce a structural bias toward high density regions, often triggering mode collapse. We introduce Contact unbalanced Optimal Transport (CuOT), a novel uOT formulation grounded in contact geometry that decouples density transport direction from mass growth to eliminate structural bias. This allows CuOT to adapt sampling steps to the local data structure, naturally reducing step sizes in high-density regions to prevent overshooting and ensuring stable convergence. The result is a stable, scalable path-length minimization solver that consistently outperforms WFR-based methods on biological dynamics reconstruction, unpaired image-to-image translation, and video generation.

With the same source nodes and class weight, sampled graph views are not distinguishable from plain copies of those nodes. We recommend tuning baselines, repeating labelled splits, reporting organic and injected anomalies separately, and checking label-free degree and feature-norm baselines.

Abstract: Anytime Automatic Algorithm Selection (AAAS) aims to make solver choices under varying time budgets. The existing AAAS approaches largely rely on static instance characteristics and make limited use of temporal information revealed during solver execution. We propose ST-GNN-AAAS, a Spatio-Temporal Graph Neural Network framework for AAAS in the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP). The framework represents evolving incumbent tours using time-varying node features, including distances from each node to its neighbors in the current tour, and combines these features with solver performance observations over a short history window. Given this short history, ST-GNN-AAAS predicts solver selections over a longer future horizon. We evaluate the method on a subset of diverse TSP benchmarks from NATIONAL, TNM, TSPLIB, and VLSI, releasing the corresponding solver execution data to support reproducibility. Abstract: Foundation models are built through large-scale pretraining, often followed by post-training, and are then adapted to specific tasks and behaviors. But, does the adaptation add truly new capabilities, or the base model already holds it? We decompose the update induced by different adaptations into a component shared across all inputs and a residual that varies with the input. We find that the shared component suffices for most of the adaptation gain and can be learned directly: a shift adapter that learns a constant vector per layer, identical for every input, reproduces the behavior of LoRA and full fine-tuning. We call this the potentiality principle of foundation models: the base model already contains the capacity needed for task-specific expertise. To adapt to a new task, the model just needs to be actualized, i.e., pushed in a certain direction via a constant, input-independent shift. We show the implications of this principle on parameter-efficient adaptation, steering, and convergence across models, and how it explains the neural thickets phenomenon.

</details>

Abstract: Neural networks in continual learning often lose plasticity: some neurons become inactive, while others fire almost constantly. This limits adaptation to shifting data and wastes capacity. Prior work mitigates this by periodically reinitializing low-utility units, but such resets can destroy previously learned features and do not proactively prevent low utility. We study a simple diagnostic measure: the firing rate of ReLU units, defined as the fraction of positive pre-activations. Low rates identify dead units, while very high rates indicate linearized, always-on units. Based on this view, we introduce a lightweight load-balancing mechanism that adjusts per-neuron thresholds to keep firing rates within a target range. Across continual ImageNet and class-incremental CIFAR-100, firing-rate distributions explain differences in plasticity across a range of approaches, including well-known techniques such as L2 regularization and non-affine normalization, and load balancing matches or exceeds reset-based methods on both benchmarks. The same mechanism carries over to reinforcement learning, improving Rainbow DQN on Atari over a LayerNorm baseline.

</details>

The proxies must satisfy exclusion restrictions and be complete, meaning that they reflect every aspect of the confounder. In practice, they are a few variables hand-picked from a structured table, even though clinical notes and physiological waveforms carry rich information about the confounder. Such records are too high-dimensional for a proximal estimator, and generic compression such as PCA may keep modality-specific variation (style) instead of the confounder, destroying completeness. Our key observation is that, under the proximal condition, the confounder is the only link between a treatment-side and an outcome-side modality beyond the covariates. Learning what they share therefore captures the confounder and filters out modality-specific style. Contrastive Proxy Forests (CPF) learns both proxies by contrasting the two modalities, given the covariates, on a separate unlabeled sample, and passes them to a proximal causal forest. This contrastive objective has an exact closed-form solution, ridge canonical correlation analysis. We prove that CPF preserves the exclusion restrictions exactly and that its proxies inherit the completeness of the raw records under conditions we characterize. Where style is strong, CPF lowers the error of conditional effects by 10-22% relative to PCA proxies, and on MIMIC-IV it is the only method whose point estimate of warfarin's effect on stroke falls inside the range of randomized trials.

</details>

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of learning robust coarse correlated equilibria (CCE) in finite-horizon, general-sum, distributionally robust Markov games with known rewards and a generative model of the nominal transitions. Each player evaluates a policy by its worst-case return over total-variation (TV) balls centered at the nominal transition distribution of every stage, state, and joint action; this is the standard $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set. Existing generative-model guarantees for this model scale with the number $\prod_iA_i$ of joint actions, where $A_i$ is the number of actions of player~$i$. Since this number grows exponentially with the number of players, these guarantees suffer from the curse of multiagency. We design an algorithm whose sample complexity depends on the action sets only through the largest individual action-set size $A_*:=\max_iA_i$. The algorithm uses $\widetilde O(SA_*H^5D^4/\varepsilon^4)$ transition samples and, with probability at least $1-δ$, returns a Markov policy that is an $\varepsilon$-robust CCE from every initial state. Here $S$ is the number of states, $H$ is the horizon, and $D:=\max_i\min\{H,1/ρ_i\}$, where $ρ_i$ is the TV radius of player~$i$. Two ingredients make this possible: a plug-in estimate of the robust backup whose mean error does not depend on the number of states, and a single batch of samples per round that serves as bandit feedback for all players at once. The result shows that the dependence on $\prod_iA_i$ can be avoided without modifying the $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set.

</details>

Abstract: Ptychography provides high-resolution lensless imaging across X-ray, electron, and optical microscopy, but reconstructing images from diffraction measurements generally requires computationally intensity iterative phase retrieval. These methods often require experiment-specific analysis, limiting real-time feedback as data acquisition rates increase. Here, we demonstrate PtychoFM, a physics-aware foundation model for ptychographic imaging that combines large-scale pre-training with experiment-specific fine-tuning to reconstruct complex object images directly from individual diffraction patterns. At a synchrotron beamline, diffraction frames were streamed from the detector to a single GPU for inference at up to 200 frames per second and returned for real-time visualization. PtychoFM provides a practical route to rapid, transferable ptychographic reconstruction and live experimental feedback under nonideal conditions.

Abstract: Deepfake detection benchmarks are commonly designed to evaluate whether models can distinguish authentic from manipulated audiovisual content. However, benchmark construction pipelines may introduce unintended associations between class labels and simple file-level properties. In this work, we identify a potential file-size bias in two widely used audio-visual deepfake datasets. In the 1M-Deepfakes Detection Challenge dataset, a score derived from file size alone achieves 70.13% AUC on the validation set. When file size is considered together with video duration, simple file-level methods exceed 90% AUC on 1M and 85% AUC on FakeAVCeleb. Although the same pattern is not observed in DFDC, the results suggest that the observed association is dataset-dependent rather than an inherent property of deepfake videos. We discuss several possible explanations, including differences in encoding, compression, source data, manipulation procedures, and post-processing, although the present analysis does not establish the underlying cause. We further discuss whether the factors associated with file size may also leave traces in decoded video or audio, and consequently whether they could influence content-based deepfake detection, as an important direction for future work. Our findings highlight the importance of examining simple file-level properties when constructing and evaluating deepfake datasets and benchmarks.

Abstract: Broadly speaking, reinforcement learning (RL) post-training converts compute into model capabilities by training on model-generated data. Typically, the rollouts are discarded after each update, and the knowledge acquired during RL resides in the model weights. In this work, we investigate how much of this knowledge is retained in the rollouts themselves. A natural first attempt is to replay the original RL algorithm on archived rather than freshly generated rollouts, but this leads to catastrophic collapse. We provide a simple explanation for this phenomenon, which in turn suggests a remedy: maximum likelihood on the archived rollouts. Surprisingly, starting from the same base model and replaying the rollouts in the order in which they were generated, without any distinction between correct and incorrect responses, is sufficient to recover the performance of online RL. In fact, a single maximum likelihood update per rollout batch closely tracks the entire learning curve of online RL. Furthermore, maximum likelihood on the rollouts of the last few RL steps often suffices to recover the final model's performance. This suggests that much of the compute invested in RL goes into discovering an improved sampling distribution rather than learning it from samples.

</details>

We propose a Unified Framework for Efficient Contrastive Alignment via Kernels (UniCon), which spans linear and nonlinear encoders as well as one-to-one and many-to-many alignments. At its core, UniCon introduces the contrastive similarity weight matrix $S(γ)$, which enables closed-form global solutions that provably replace minibatch back-propagation with exact updates. Through the lens of reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS), UniCon provides a kernelized perspective that unifies contrastive alignment and reveals its connection to spectral methods. To validate the theory, we conduct experiments on synthetic, unimodal, multimodal, and zero-shot tasks, demonstrating that UniCon achieves substantial efficiency gains while preserving generality and strong empirical performance.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models effectively learn action policies from robot demonstrations, but struggle to generalize to changes in object positions and task goals. General multimodal foundation models (MFMs) offer complementary scene understanding and spatial reasoning, but remain slow and unreliable for direct fine-grained control. To understand how MFMs can improve VLA spatial generalization, we conduct a systematic capability analysis across multiple VLA architectures using atomic-stage interventions, revealing that many failures stem from poor target grounding and global spatial routing, while local manipulation skills remain largely usable. Based on these findings, we propose MFM-VLA, a selective collaboration framework in which an MFM provides target grounding and spatial guidance, controllers execute free-space motion, and the VLA retains control over fine-grained manipulation. We fine-tune the VLA with action-chunk-level dense geometric captions and perturbed-state recovery data to improve local guidance following and recovery from unfamiliar handoff states. A development-calibrated capability router selects between direct VLA execution and MFM-assisted execution to avoid unnecessary intervention. Across LIBERO-PRO, ALOHA-Sim-PRO, RoboCasa365, and real-world chemistry tasks, MFM-VLA improves success rates by 14.9, 74.0, 3.6, and 30.9 percentage points, respectively, over the corresponding base VLA. Abstract: Recent advances in video generation have made automated advertising production increasingly feasible. However, an effective advertisement must faithfully represent the product and communicate its selling points through compelling visual storytelling, which requires more than visual synthesis: it calls for deeper product understanding and strategic decisions about how to present product value. Crucially, product research and creative design are closely intertwined, as the information gathered about a product determines which features can be faithfully demonstrated and how they should be presented. In this paper, we introduce AdProducer, the first end-to-end agentic framework for advertising video creation, transforming concise creative briefs into finished advertisements. Our multimodal agent autonomously researches products through web and image search and translates its findings into selling-point-driven storyboards that guide a frozen video generator. To achieve this, we develop a dedicated data construction pipeline that yields two training datasets, AdProducer-SFT and AdProducer-RL, and train the agent through supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with joint optimization of storyboard- and video-level rewards. We further introduce AdCreation-Bench, comprising 305 advertising briefs across eight domains, with two complementary metrics: PG-Score for product correctness and creative execution, and Ad-Score for overall advertising effectiveness assessed by an independent judge. Experiments demonstrate that AdProducer improves the PG-Score of MiniMax H3 from 17.3 to 51.3 and generalizes to Veo 3.1 without additional training, improving its score from 28.0 to 35.9, with consistent gains in Ad-Score, VideoScore2, and human evaluations. Code, models, and data are released.

Many cameras store a portrait shot sideways, with an orientation tag telling viewers how to turn it, and this tag can be ignored, lost or wrong. We measure how rotated photos affect five such models on three datasets. A few rotated photos lower camera pose accuracy for every model, and the sideways photos already in a standard benchmark do the same. Even the most robust model still loses accuracy. The failure is also silent: the models' confidence does not reliably flag it, and most official image loaders ignore the tag. The fixes we test each correct only part of the problem. These findings call for orientation checks in reconstruction pipelines and benchmarks.

Abstract: Verifying image authenticity is a critical need for a variety of applications and decision-making in the remote sensing (RS) domain, yet it is becoming more challenging as advancements in generative AI enable producing increasingly realistic synthetic overhead data. However, existing RS deepfake benchmarks prioritize sheer volume at the expense of visual realism and diversity, while also lacking rigorous out-of-distribution (OOD) testing protocols to evaluate real-world robustness. To address this, we introduce the Geospatial Deepfakes Dataset (GDD), a benchmark for RS deepfake detection and manipulation localization. Rather than relying on uncurated, redundant synthetic generation, GDD comprises images across diverse modern generative models and real image sources, alongside manipulations from diffusion inpainting, splicing, and copy-move operations. Crucially, GDD establishes an OOD evaluation protocol for deepfake detection comprising unseen real image sources and generative models. We benchmark five deepfake detectors and two localization models across intra- and cross-dataset regimes and reveal that detectors trained on high-volume, uncurated benchmarks overfit to dataset-specific artifacts, whereas training on GDD yields superior cross-dataset generalization despite its compact scale. This benchmarking also exposes critical failure modes in current state-of-the-art methods. Combined, GDD and its evaluation protocol establish a high-quality standard for developing and stress-testing detection and localization models in this safety-critical RS domain. The dataset is publicly available.

Abstract: Cognitive neuroscience learned that an activation map is not a map of necessity. Regions that light up during a task are not always needed for it, and only lesions reveal which ones are. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) vision-language models come with an activation map for free, since the router records which experts respond to image and text tokens, and recent work reads modality-specialized experts off this map. We bring the lesion. In Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B, Kimi-VL-A3B and Gemma-4-26B-A4B, 3 to 12% of the experts are vision-exclusive: image tokens route to them at up to five times the uniform rate and text tokens almost never. In Qwen3-VL they receive a quarter of all image-token routing, and lesioning all of them costs less than one percentage point on average over eight perception benchmarks and free-form OCR, no more than lesioning a random set of the same size. In Kimi-VL and Gemma-4 the lesion also costs no more than a random set. Scoped lesions, which remove an expert set only at image positions or only at text positions, then show where the models do depend on their experts. In Qwen3-VL, silencing every expert at every image position costs 3 points, against 45 at text positions, where the dependence sits in dual-use experts that text-only tasks need as well. Across models the image-side dependence is 2 to 3 points in Qwen3-VL and Kimi-VL and 26 in Gemma-4. Reading text in images is the one ability that needs image-position experts in every model. The exclusive experts receive informative patches, produce outputs of ordinary size, and stay inert under sensory deprivation: text-only training recruits them only when load balancing forces it. In Kimi-VL the balancing bias itself manufactures most of them. Load balancing draws the modality map in the part of the computation the models depend on least. Expert selection, pruning and skipping should follow the lesion, not the map.

</details>

We introduce GoldiBench, a benchmark of 500 editing tasks in five professional applications (darktable, RawTherapee, GIMP, Inkscape, Blender), solvable by writing a program or by operating the GUI. Each task pairs a START image with a TARGET rendered from a hidden program, so the amount is fixed by a known target; the agent's edit is re-rendered and compared with TARGET, and the same task is posed with the tools and values, the tools only, or the goal only, separating operation from effect judgement. Across 25 open, GUI and closed models, agents operate well (99-100% with programs from 4B up when told the values) but fall off a cliff when they must judge the amount: given only the tools, the best model passes 19% of the tasks with programs and 33% through the GUI, and scale does not close the gap. Manuals barely help; the measured TARGET-START difference more than doubles the pass rate, but the same difference shown as an image does not for open models. Allowed four renders, agents almost always stop after the first; forced to compare, the strong models double their pass rate. Agents know the tools, but neither read nor check the amount.

Abstract: Robotic manipulation requires predicting how robot-object interactions evolve over time, encompassing both visual appearance and scene geometry. In multi-camera settings, such predictions need to capture the evolving 3D scene consistently across viewpoints, while supporting controllable generation under different robot actions and camera configurations. Existing video generation models provide strong priors for visual appearance and motion but typically lack explicit geometric modeling. Meanwhile, recent advances in geometry-aware and multi-view generation have explored joint appearance-geometry modeling and cross-view consistency, yet generating temporally coherent appearance and geometry across multiple viewpoints under robotic manipulation conditions remains challenging. In this work, we propose GeoMVGen, a framework for joint multi-view RGB and pointmap video generation in robotic manipulation. Built on a video diffusion transformer, our framework employs modality-specific adaptation modules to capture the distinct characteristics of appearance and geometry, while using cross-modal residual connections to facilitate information exchange between the two modalities. To establish geometric correspondences across viewpoints, we further introduce a cross-view projection mechanism with complementary geometric supervisions, combining pointmap consistency with geometry foundation model-guided feature alignment. These designs enable controllable generation of temporally evolving multi-view observations with coherent appearance and geometrically consistent scene structures. Extensive experiments on 4D video generation dataset comprising simulated and real-world robotic manipulation scenarios demonstrate that GeoMVGen outperforms state-of-the-art methods in video quality, geometric accuracy, and cross-view consistency.

</details>

Abstract: Describing an image requires not only recognizing what is visible but deciding what should be inferred. We investigate a systematic gap between humans and VLMs in their tendency to attribute visible actions or events to people who are not present in an image. We term this behavior unwarranted person attribution bias and find that, across 25 SOTA VLMs, captions of person-absent scenes mention a person four times as often as human-written captions of the same images. Through a preference study, we further show that both humans and VLMs prefer captions that avoid such attributions. Motivated by this, we introduce NOBODY, a preference dataset designed to bridge the gap between humans and VLMs in unwarranted person attribution. Through direct preference optimization (DPO), we align open VLMs on NOBODY and show that their agreement with human captions on person attribution reaches or exceeds inter-human agreement, whereas nine existing public preference datasets leave this behavior largely unchanged. Furthermore, we show that human judges preferred the aligned VLM captions significantly more than those of the baseline model. Alongside NOBODY, we release NOBODY-Bench, which evaluates VLMs for unwarranted person attribution bias and their alignment against human captions and preference. Abstract: Harnesses for vision-language models (VLMs) have grown with the models' capabilities, notably for images far larger than those seen in training, where downscaling loses small details. Following the recursive inference of Recursive Language Models, the VLM programmatically controls its own visual context in a code REPL that holds the full-resolution image and calls itself recursively on regions that it chooses. With a cost-efficient frontier model as the base of every method, RVLM-X is more accurate than a single call on all 7 public benchmarks and has the highest pooled accuracy. There, RVLM-X answers 71.4% of held-out questions against 55.5% for the strongest baseline. Overall, our findings suggest that as VLMs plan and use tools more reliably, letting the model write its own visual search works better than fixing one in advance.

Abstract: Recent time-series foundation models (TSFMs) forecast a distribution over futures, and multimodal time-series (TS) forecasting further exploits other modalities to improve forecasting accuracy. However, existing works have two limitations: 1) TSFMs reduce the predictive distribution to its median or mean, flattening diverse futures into a calm line, and 2) multimodal TS forecasting models incorporate text into the forecaster through TS-language alignment, which is substantially weaker than vision-language alignment. To address these limitations, we propose the VIsion-language SelectOR (VISOR), a multimodal TS forecasting framework that selects one plausible future instead of averaging across candidates, leveraging the alignment capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs) to select the most suitable candidate based on textual information. VISOR consists of two components: 1) a text-free stochastic predictor that samples diverse candidate futures, and 2) a VLM selector that reads the candidates as a single chart image and selects one based on the text. To avoid overly calm forecasting paths, we train the selector with a shape-aware target that evaluates each candidate based on both point-wise error and path shape. Across three benchmarks covering ten datasets, VISOR achieves the best average ranking in both point and volatility errors compared with TSFMs and multimodal TS forecasting models. Abstract: Interactive video world models generate navigable worlds frame by frame, but a persistent world requires previously observed scenes to remain recoverable as users move away and return. Existing memory evaluations, however, often focus on seen-view revisits and full rollouts, leaving cross-view scene recovery underexplored while entangling memory with trajectory-following errors and accumulated generation degradation. We introduce SceneMemBench, a benchmark for scene memory in interactive video world models. It evaluates two complementary dimensions: seen-view recall versus novel-view recovery, and controlled diagnosis versus end-to-end interaction. We introduce Continued Rollout from a shared ground-truth observation history, alongside Full Rollout over complete model-generated interactions. SceneMemBench contains 600 controlled trajectories across 33 Unreal Engine scenes and 60 Full Rollout cases, covering eight representative open- and closed-source models. We evaluate scene recovery along subject consistency, appearance fidelity, and geometric consistency. Results show that novel-view recovery is generally more challenging than seen-view recall, while model profiles under controlled histories can differ markedly from those observed in full interaction. Together, these findings reveal complementary failure modes obscured by seen-view revisits or end-to-end rollouts alone.

Abstract: Reconstructing the 3D poses of hands and objects from a single image, together with the contact between them, is fundamental to understanding manipulation. To be practical, the reconstruction must generalize to unseen objects, and the contact must be localized precisely. Yet joint feed-forward estimators often rely on predefined object sets, while contact is typically predicted from image features alone, without the explicit geometry needed to localize it. To address both limitations, we present HORCRUX, a unified feed-forward transformer with coarse-to-fine contact estimation. For unseen objects, a frozen foundation model encodes the object from a given or image-reconstructed mesh rather than from object-specific knowledge. A shared transformer then reasons jointly over the object and one or two hands to keep them aligned. For precise contact, coarse per-joint predictions from latent features are resolved over finer hand regions using the reconstructed geometry. Across seven benchmarks, HORCRUX achieves state-of-the-art object and hand pose accuracy among joint estimators and outperforms a dedicated contact estimator in fine-grained contact. These results suggest that contact is best estimated jointly with reconstruction, whose geometry directly localizes it.

Abstract: Where a person looks indicates their focus of attention, and how their gaze shifts between targets reveals how that attention responds to a changing scene. Existing gaze target estimation methods largely operate frame by frame and do not model these shifts. Gaze shifts are often driven by dynamic changes in the scene, which is the kind of information world models are designed to encode. This relationship is subtle, however, since an event far across the frame may produce only a slight movement of the person's head or eyes. We therefore introduce the Gaze World Model (GWM), which co-trains latent scene prediction with person-conditioned gaze supervision on a frozen V-JEPA 2.1 encoder; in our ablations, the latent prediction objective improves both shift detection and gaze localization. Given a video clip and the person's head box, GWM estimates gaze locations and detects gaze shifts in the observed clip, and forecasts future gaze and shifts from predicted scene features. To train and evaluate these tasks, we augment VideoAttentionTarget and ChildPlay with event-level gaze-shift annotations. GWM achieves state-of-the-art shift detection on these benchmarks, about 10 points higher in four-class AP than the strongest baseline, while remaining comparable to the strongest image-based models in gaze target estimation, and outperforms the compared baselines on future gaze localization and shift forecasting.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures learn by predicting features of masked regions from visible context. In chest computed tomography, the findings that decide a diagnosis, such as a small nodule, are often not predictable from the surrounding anatomy. A hidden finding is then best predicted toward normal anatomy, and the prediction loss stops rewarding the encoder for representing it. We call this the context shortcut. Conditioning the predictor on the radiology report repairs the prediction, but in our setup the encoder gains little over image-only pretraining: the report supplies what the encoder never had to learn. JECT therefore gives the report two roles: a condition for the predictor and, through masked section-text prediction, a target for the encoder, which must predict the report's anatomical sections from the unmasked scan. A hierarchical three-dimensional encoder that predicts at every stage keeps this clinical content at every scale. In ablations, the text target improves classification and localization, while conditioning adds little to the encoder. Trained from scratch on 173k volumes, the recipe reaches the level of current computed tomography foundation models in classification, retrieval and nodule malignancy, and of task-specific models in lung-cancer risk prediction after fine-tuning. Conditioning still matters: its predictor can be reused, with no extra training, to show where a report sentence acts, changing most at the finding the sentence describes. Code and pretrained weights are available.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models need to continually acquire new manipulation skills while retaining the ability to follow previously learned instructions. Experience replay (ER) can mitigate catastrophic forgetting, but retaining only a small number of historical expert frames per task leaves many old-instruction responses without direct supervision. Under limited replay, we observe a structured failure mode in which an earlier instruction can trigger a subsequently learned, competing goal. To address this gap, we propose Cross-Instruction Anchoring (XIA), which supplements expert replay with instruction-conditioned response preservation. At each task boundary, XIA freezes the previous policy as a teacher and matches its responses to old instructions paired with current observations. This extends supervision beyond stored historical examples without requiring additional expert replay data. Experiments across multiple VLA architectures and three LIBERO suites demonstrate improved success rates and reduced forgetting compared with ER under identical historical expert-data budgets. Additional RoboCasa and real-world experiments further support the effectiveness of XIA.

Abstract: For endoscopic robotic surgery, accurate dense depth perception serves as a foundational capability, which enables navigation, instrument localization, and tissue interaction. However, endoscopic video lacks reliable metric depth, while metric measurements obtained via tracking, time-of-flight sensing, or instrument contact only cover a small fraction of pixels in each frame, rendering sparse depth completion an urgent and unsolved challenge. In this work, we propose Temporal Anchor Densification with Reliability-Aware Memory (TANDEM), a causal sparse depth completion framework for endoscopic video. It adopts a lightweight design and leverages the video stream: past measurements re-projected into the current view recover the metric evidence unavailable within a single sparse frame. To keep this evidence interpretable, accurate, and efficient, an explicit memory scores each re-projected measurement with analytic re-observation, age, and motion-parallax factors without overriding current measurements. A compact network then reconstructs depth via reliability-weighted gradient integration and a frequency-gated reference prior. We evaluate TANDEM on the C3VD colonoscopy and StereoMIS laparoscopy datasets. With only 5.58 M parameters, TANDEM reduces the root mean square error by 11.7% and 32.0%, respectively, compared with the strongest baseline, and achieves state-of-the-art accuracy. It also remains accurate under pose errors of the magnitude reported for endoscopic pose estimation. Code will be released at https://github.com/Wk-cpu-art/TANDEM upon acceptance.

Pointwise fusion outperforms independent VLM and geometry streams on both benchmarks. In closed-loop driving on 420 HUGSIM scenarios, VLG-Drive outperforms all compared planners. Together, these contributions establish a foundation for learning and evaluating the instrument-tissue interactions underlying future surgical automation.

Abstract: Generating videos with physically plausible dynamics remains challenging despite advances in visual quality. Latent world models offer predictive signals for evaluating physical plausibility, but higher agreement between predicted and observed features may reflect easier-to-predict scene content rather than more plausible dynamics. We propose \textbf{S}elf-Referenced \textbf{P}hysical \textbf{A}lignment \textbf{R}ewards (\textbf{SPAR}), a framework that uses world-model predictions for inference-time physical alignment. SPAR compares each video's prediction error with that of static clips constructed by repeating its own frames, providing a content-matched reference for evaluating generated dynamics. To account for the uneven spatial and temporal distribution of physical interactions, we introduce a unified \textbf{spatiotemporal reward refinement} strategy. \textbf{Spatial refinement} emphasizes localized prediction discrepancies, while \textbf{temporal refinement} moderates brief error peaks and supplements prediction errors with agreement between predicted and observed feature changes. Evaluations across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that SPAR improves the physical plausibility of both \textbf{text-to-video generation} and \textbf{image-to-video continuation}, bringing generated dynamics into closer alignment with the physical regularities governing real-world events. Additional results and resources are available at https://gavin-jy.github.io/spar-page/

Abstract: Vision foundation models can precisely segment objects based solely on a text prompt. However, they struggle to recover objects that are small, occluded or whose appearance is poorly captured by the original textual description. Vision language models can examine the image and its context, they can propose richer descriptions of the objects to be detected. But these models struggle with precise segmentation and localization. We introduce Visual Thinking, a training-free framework that combines a vision-language model with Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM 3) through an iterative active perception loop. At each iteration, the VLM proposes short noun phrases using the image, candidate crops and history from past iterations. A controller ranks these phrases using embedding similarity between the original prompt and the proposal, an entropy-based verification priority and the novelty of the prompt. Next, SAM 3 uses prompts to detect objects in the image. We associate detections across iterations and update each object's confidence score using a weighted mean of scores obtained from SAM 3 with a larger weight for detections using narrower prompts. We supply confident detections as positive visual exemplars for SAM 3, and incorporate image tiling to recover small objects in cluttered images. We evaluate counting and localization on CARPK, FSCD-147, PixMo-Count, and OmniCount (Fruits). Compared with SAM 3, Visual Thinking reduces the counting mean absolute error (MAE) by about 18 on FSC-147 and about 8 on CARPK. It improves box average precision (AP) by more than 12 on FSCD-147 and CARPK. Visual Thinking, with fixed or no tiling, performs on par with Claude Opus 5.5 at extra-high reasoning effort, while running on single desktop GPU in about 5.91 seconds per image, compared to about 46.53 seconds per image for Claude through its API.

Abstract: Modeling 4D hand-object interaction (HOI) is fundamental to understanding manipulation. This requires physics, since hands move objects only by touch and each material responds differently. Yet existing methods ignore this physics or simulate only a narrow range of matter. Programs can instead describe any matter with its physics, but coding agents omit the hand that drives the interaction. We close this gap with CodeHOI, to our knowledge the first agentic framework that writes physically plausible 4D HOI as programs. To write one, an Object Agent first builds each object with the physics of its matter. An HOI Agent then specifies the hands by their contacts and simulates each kind of matter with an established physics solver. Finally, an Evaluator Agent checks each result against physical laws. We also introduce CodeHOI-Bench with 100 reconstruction and generation tasks across four kinds of matter. With off-the-shelf frontier models, CodeHOI writes interactions that are plausible for every kind of matter and remain so when transferred to an independent physics engine. It thereby enables coding agents to write physically plausible HOI across diverse matter.

</details>

Abstract: Even when multimodal large language models (MLLMs) answer incorrectly, their internal cues often point to the relevant region. A widely used remedy re-presents the cue-selected region as an enlarged crop to supply finer detail. However, we find that much of its gain requires no new detail: it largely survives when the crop comes from the already-seen low-resolution input, and partly even when the region's cached tokens are merely copied. Part of re-presentation's gain thus comes from drawing attention to evidence the model already holds. We propose InCue, a test-time framework that makes better use of this evidence without additional visual input. InCue treats visual attention and predictive-entropy gradients as Internal Cues, couples them into a spatial target, and optimizes only an offset on the existing visual tokens to steer attention toward it. Under matched visual-token budgets, InCue achieves state-of-the-art performance, consistently outperforming recent test-time methods and improving four MLLMs by up to 8.9% on V$^*$ and 23.4% on its hard samples. Its gains carry over to eight general benchmarks. Code will be available at https://github.com/paul-swu/InCue.

Abstract: Comparing two entities in an image, multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) often answer wrongly. We find that the answer bias enters not when the model sees, but when it decides with the image in view: the model's own descriptions of the two entities often already hold the right answer. We therefore propose D$^3$ (Describe, Don't Decide): the model describes each entity alone, decides from these descriptions without the image, and merges this decision with its direct answer. Without any training, D$^3$ improves all 79 model-benchmark pairs across ten benchmarks and eight 7-9B MLLMs. On held-out GQA comparison questions, the mean accuracy of these models rises from 72.4% to 83.2% (up to +21.5% per model), higher than that of Qwen2.5-VL-32B answering directly (82.8%). Abstract: Latent action models (LAMs) learn action-like latents from unlabeled video by coupling frame-pair inference with latent-conditioned prediction, enabling policy and world-model pretraining. However, what the latent must explain depends on the representation space used for action inference and transition prediction. When this space entangles the agent's evolving physical state with visual context, such as background and persistent agent appearance, inferred latents can encode information unrelated to the agent's actions. To address this challenge, we propose Agent-State LAM, which jointly learns a compact agent-state representation and latent actions modeling its transitions. The compact state aggregates dense visual features into a shared space for both action inference and forward prediction. To guide what this state retains, we introduce spatially matched temporal discrimination (SMTD), which trains the state to distinguish its source frame from nearby frames through spatially flexible matching with frozen local visual features. Rather than comparing global frame representations, SMTD scores each frame by emphasizing its strongest local feature matches across spatial locations. This scoring allows background and persistent agent appearance to remain matched across frames despite displacement, making these shared cues less informative for identifying the source frame and encouraging the representation to capture the agent's evolving physical state. Extensive experiments on distracting control tasks and six real robot video datasets demonstrate improved action recovery, downstream control, and world-model prediction. Code and checkpoints are available at https://github.com/Mid-Push/Agent-State-LAM.

</details>

Abstract: Specialist medical vision-language models (VLMs) are strong within their specialties but hard to unify: their training data are often private, and independently developed specialists cannot be assumed to share an architecture. Deploying these large models separately is costly. We present HuluOPD, an on-policy distillation recipe that unifies heterogeneous specialists into one lightweight VLM using only unlabeled prompts, without requiring reference answers or the specialists' training data. The lightweight model answers each prompt, and the specialist of the prompt's modality supervises its response; a continuation-aligned cross-tokenizer loss makes this possible even when they differ in architecture and tokenizer. We apply HuluOPD to five specialists, for ultrasound, pathology, optical coherence tomography, fundus photography, and dental imaging. To distill and evaluate them, we build HuluOPDCorpus, 914k unlabeled prompts, and HuluOPDBench, 47k held-out test items, from over 90 public datasets, and score all models under one protocol on 132k test items, including third-party benchmarks. A single model with 10% of the five specialists' combined parameters retains 94% of their question-answering performance, and one with 2% retains 89%. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of unifying heterogeneous medical specialist VLMs without requiring their training data or a shared architecture.

Abstract: Bi-temporal satellite imagery is central to disaster assessment: deciding what was damaged requires comparing a pre-event scene with its post-event counterpart. A vision-language model (VLM) can nonetheless appear to answer change questions while relying mainly on the post-event image or on the prompt's temporal framing. We present a controlled diagnostic of temporal grounding and apply it to one lightweight VLM, InternVL3-2B, on DisasterM3: 1,659 optical and SAR image pairs from 41 events and nine disaster types, in event-disjoint partitions, with 9,031 reasoning questions. We compare post-only input, the true pre/post pair, the post image repeated twice, reversed image order, and an unrelated pre-event image. The true pair scores $+0.5$ accuracy points above the repeated-post control (95\% paired bootstrap CI $[-0.2, +1.2]$), and reversing the order changes accuracy by $-0.2$ points ($[-0.8, +0.5]$). Under this protocol we therefore find no measurable benefit from the content of the true pre-event image and little sensitivity to temporal order. The model is not blind to the first image, however: the true pair scores $2.5$ points above a pair whose pre-event image comes from another event ($[+1.6, +3.4]$). State-transition and multi-hop questions remain hard, and optical and SAR inputs show different profiles. We outline temporal scene graphs as a testable next step, not a validated solution, and argue that temporal benchmarks for Earth observation should report whether answers depend on the intended evidence, not only how often they are right.

Abstract: The realistic rendering of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has drawn interest from real-time applications that require maps to be built online. This led to coupling 3DGS with SLAM, providing camera poses and initial Gaussians for the map. However, the map is still optimized with designs inherited from conventional SLAM, which train only on keyframes selected for localization and regularize geometry with uncertain frontend depth. These designs overlook the model complexity of a 3DGS map: with millions of parameters but few views of each region, the map overfits to sparse inputs. Resolving this is challenging, as every view and Gaussian added to the map competes for the same real-time budget. We close this gap from both sides. To add views while keyframes preserve the structure of the map, we progressively admit non-keyframe images as mapping updates complete and allocate the limited updates across different groups of frames according to the supervision each group provides. To control the model complexity of the map, edge-guided initialization places new Gaussians where image detail requires them. To complement the uncertain frontend depth, Self-Referenced Opacity Regularization (SOR) uses accumulated rendering evidence, at no extra rendering cost, to suppress the opacity of Gaussians that persistently contribute in front of a map-derived surface reference. Together, these designs improve both the appearance and the geometry of online maps within the real-time budget.

</details>

Abstract: Occupancy world models predict future 3D scenes conditioned on ego actions, yet their predictions may not faithfully follow the specified trajectory under counterfactual actions. Because driving datasets provide sparse coverage of alternative trajectories for each scene, existing models learn the spatial effect of ego motion implicitly from observed scene--trajectory combinations, which may entangle action effects with scene--trajectory correlations. We propose ReCast, an ego-trajectory-guided transformer that grounds future prediction in action-induced geometric correspondence across ego-centric frames. ReCast decouples historical encoding from future decoding and aligns deformable interactions using relative ego transformations, while retaining flexibility for non-rigid scene evolution. We also introduce a counterfactual action-faithfulness benchmark covering in-distribution, tail, and out-of-distribution trajectories. On Occ3D-nuScenes, ReCast achieves competitive standard forecasting performance while improving counterfactual forecasting, with particularly clear gains under out-of-distribution trajectories.

</details>

Abstract: Surgical scene understanding is constrained by data scarcity and long-tail class imbalance, as annotating surgical videos demands scarce clinical expertise while rare procedural events remain underrepresented. We tackle this through data generation and propose SurgGenesis, a generative surgical world model that learns future surgical state transitions in a latent space from visual observations and textual procedural descriptions, serving as a scalable surgical data engine. To adapt a large-scale video diffusion model to the data-scarce surgical domain without catastrophic forgetting, we introduce a three-stage progressive LoRA strategy that incrementally injects endoscopic visual priors, procedural semantics, and spatial motion cues. Rather than judging generation by visual fidelity alone, we validate SurgGenesis along two axes that separate a world model from a generic generator: (i) future-awareness, by comparing predicted futures against the real future through a GT-latent prediction gap and temporal-horizon analysis, and (ii) action-faithful usefulness, by checking that generated clips contain the commanded surgical triplet and that using them to rebalance long-tail events and drive downstream understanding (segmentation, triplet recognition, 3D reconstruction) yields action-specific gains. The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses large vision-language models (LVLMs) by calibrating quantization parameters on a small dataset without retraining. However, despite increasingly sophisticated calibration strategies, prior methods suffer substantial performance degradation under low-bit weight--activation quantization. To understand this degradation, we locate where quantization error is concentrated and examine the corresponding weight and activation distributions. We observe that a few outliers dominate the ranges shared by per-channel (PC) weights and per-token (PT) activations, leaving insufficient resolution for most values and mapping many of them to zero. These findings motivate a shift in focus from calibration refinement to the underlying quantization representation. We therefore present Rotate-and-Refine Quantization (RRQ) as a simple, effective baseline for LVLM PTQ, combining local Hadamard transforms with group quantization and efficient group-aware execution. Across 8 multimodal tasks and diverse quantization settings, RRQ substantially outperforms baselines while reducing inference time and memory usage. At W4A4, it retains over 96\% of full-precision performance on 8B models and over 99\% on a 32B model, compared with less than 8\% for these baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/kmc3661/RRQ

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents translate screenshots and user instructions into executable actions. Most existing approaches rely on vision-language models to generate actions as text, often leading to substantial inference latency due to autoregressive decoding. To address this inefficiency, we propose GUI-PRISM, a hybrid action decoder that directly predicts action types and structured arguments while retaining language generation for open-ended text. Unlike existing spatial decoding methods that primarily focus on target localization, GUI-PRISM extends direct prediction to action types and their associated arguments, enabling complete GUI action prediction. Specifically, GUI-PRISM employs field-specific queries to extract action-relevant semantic and spatial information from a shared multimodal feature bank. It further models dependencies among action components through conditional queries. Gated residual cross-attention refines these predictions using fine-grained multimodal information. GUI-PRISM achieves 95.1\% accuracy on ScreenSpot-v2, outperforming the strongest head-based spatial decoding method by 2.0 percentage points while delivering a 3.8$\times$ inference speedup over the baseline. On AndroidControl-v2 and MM-Mind2Web-v2, it improves complete-action accuracy by 4.8 and 0.7 percentage points while achieving 2.7$\times$ and 3.9$\times$ inference speedups over the baseline, respectively. Abstract: First-order reinforcement learning, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), is widely used to post-train Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for multimodal reasoning. However, it incurs heavy memory and system overhead from backpropagation, optimizer states, and a reference model. Forward-only zeroth-order (ZO) methods, such as Evolution Strategies (ES) and its top-k variant Augmented Random Search (ARS), avoid these overheads via reward-guided weight perturbations, yet remain unexplored for VLM reasoning. We find that directly applying standard ES or vanilla ARS to VLMs suffers from two fundamental failure modes: (i) patch-grid overfitting, fitting fragile grid-specific visual token artifacts while ignoring spatial invariants, and (ii) catastrophic forgetting, degrading general perception and out-of-distribution (OOD) tasks due to unbounded high-rank parameter and policy drift. We propose ARS-VLM, a visually aligned Augmented Random Search framework for forward-only VLM post-training. First, ARS-VLM extracts a contrastive visual basis from bilinearly paired cross-grid views, concentrating perturbations on shared visual signals while suppressing patch-shift artifacts. Second, ARS-VLM anchors shallow visual-fusion layers with a soft subspace prior while freeing deep reasoning layers, restraining policy drift without a reference model. Third, ARS-VLM scores perturbations on paired views to discard view-fragile directions during top-k selection. We evaluate ARS-VLM on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B on multimodal reasoning tasks. On 8B, ARS-VLM reaches 61.0% validation accuracy in 20 steps versus 150 for GRPO (7.5x fewer optimization steps) and avoids the late OOD collapse of vanilla ARS and ES. Moreover, ARS-VLM achieves 75.41% single-view micro accuracy across seven OOD vision reasoning benchmarks, compared with 75.31% for full-parameter GRPO, using forward-only post-training.

</details>

Abstract: People reason with pictures as well as words. Interleaved multimodal reasoning gives models a similar way to solve problems by creating intermediate images and using them to guide further reasoning. Training models with this ability usually relies on task-specific demonstrations, which are difficult to collect. We introduce ThinkMorph-SSL, a two-stage training recipe that uses images themselves as a source of supervision. The first stage, pretext reasoning, converts visual pretext tasks, such as image colorization and denoising, into instruction-free interleaved traces. Models describe a corrupted or transformed image, generate a restored or canonical image, and interpret the result. The second stage uses automatically constructed demonstrations for question-driven instruction tuning. At the same instruction-tuning budget, models trained with pretext reasoning outperform those trained with instruction tuning alone. The full recipe improves over the base model by an average of 11.33 accuracy points across twelve benchmarks. These results show that supervision derived from images can build the visual skills needed for interleaved reasoning and improve learning from task-specific demonstrations.

Abstract: Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-contrast MRI super-resolution accelerates clinical protocols by reconstructing high-resolution (HR) target modalities from low-resolution (LR) inputs, guided by easily acquired reference contrasts. While purely data-driven deep neural networks achieve strong performance via rich representation capacity, they rely on heuristic fusion mechanisms (e.g., naive concatenation or black-box cross-attention) that lack theoretical grounding. Alternatively, model-based deep unfolding networks enforce principled cross-contrast fusion through explicit priors, yet their unrolled steps remain constrained to shallow convolutions, lacking modern deep blocks to capture global context. To bridge this gap, we propose CSC-MAMBA, unifying deep-unfolded multimodal convolutional sparse coding with the Vision Mamba state-space backbone. By fusing common structural priors from the guidance modality via unfolded sparse coding and globally refining contrast-specific representations with a Mamba back- bone, our approach benefits from interpretable, model-guided fusion and long-range spatial context. Experiments on the IXI dataset demonstrate that CSC-MAMBA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in reconstruction quality, achieving up to a 0.62 dB gain in PSNR while remaining computationally efficient.

</details>
