Abstract: Representation quality in pretrained text foundation models is not concentrated at the final layer, yet practitioners routinely probe it there because layer selection without labels has remained an open problem. We present a large-scale empirical study of where layer quality lives across 25 models spanning four architectural families and four pretraining strategies, evaluated on 13 datasets across classification, pair classification, semantic textual similarity, and multiple-choice question answering. Peak probing layers depend systematically on pretraining strategy and architecture: masked encoders peak in late-middle layers, sequence-to-sequence models shift their peak with task type, contrastive sentence encoders concentrate signal at the final layer under CLS and last-token readouts but peak early under mean pooling, and decoder-only models exhibit distinct phase structure tied to depth. We distill these findings into the Stable Geometry Index (SGI), a closed-form, fully label-free selector with no fitted coefficients combining power-law fit quality, effective-rank capacity, spectral stability, and a weak depth prior. Across 383 evaluation groups SGI improves over the last-layer baseline and lifts Hit@3 from 0.39 to 0.52, recovering 40% of the gap to the oracle without using any labels, and exceeds a supervised ELMo-style layer mix on the matched subset despite using strictly less information. Our ablations also reverse a common assumption in the spectral-decay literature: power-law fit quality is a stronger predictor of layer quality than the exponent's proximity to one.

</details>

Abstract: The training of socially intelligent language models often relies on large language model (LLM) judges, which are expensive, noisy, and vulnerable to reward hacking. Multi-player social games offer a natural alternative: they require open-ended communication to coordinate with teammates and infer hidden roles, while their rules provide an objective outcome which can serve as a verifiable reward for training. Using three social games, we explore how much social intelligence Qwen3 models can acquire through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Results show that against a much stronger Gemma-3-27B model, SFT and RLVR raise the win rate of Qwen3-4B from 33% to 81% on the three trained games, and from 39% to 54% on three additional held-out games. In contrast, in-context strategies such as prompting improve the win rate of Qwen3-4B by only about 6 pp across the same games we train and evaluate on. Furthermore, we show that the gains from SFT and RLVR hold across the model sizes 1.7B, 4B, and 32B. Qualitative analyses of agent behavior show signs of stronger social intelligence; for example, trained agents tend to share information more openly, check each other's claims against public evidence, and coordinate their votes. More broadly, our results demonstrate that training LLMs on verifiable outcomes in social games can cultivate generalizable social behaviors, which offers a scalable path for building socially intelligent agents.

</details>

Abstract: Human annotations on documents (such as arrows, carets, strikethroughs, and insertions) express corrective intent yet existing OCR and document understanding systems treat these marks as visual noise rather than semantic edit operations. We introduce intent-aware OCR, the task of generating corrected transcriptions by jointly reasoning over original document content and visual intent cues (handwritten or digital) that convey edit intent. To support this setting, we present DocIntent, a multimodal dataset of 1,600 financial documents with over 5,700 annotated visual intent cues, each paired with layout-aware, intent-resolved OCR targets. We further introduce IntentBench, a 216-document benchmark designed to evaluate model robustness under diverse real-world annotation conditions. Leveraging DocIntent, we fine-tune DocIntentOCR, a suite of 3B and 7B parameter vision-language models, under direct intent supervision on corrected transcriptions. On IntentBench, the fine-tuned 3B and 7B models achieve F1-scores of 0.81 and 0.83, respectively, substantially outperforming commercial closed-source (e.g., GPT-4.1 (0.626)), and open-source models (e.g., LLaMA-3.2-Vision-90B (0.50)) demonstrating that intent-aware supervision enables compact domain-specific models to outperform substantially larger general-purpose systems on this task. This work establishes intent-aware OCR as a novel human-in-the-loop document understanding task and provides foundational resources (dataset, benchmark, models) to advance research in interactive document processing.

</details>

Abstract: Parameter-efficient adaptation of a speech foundation model to dysarthric speech requires a rarely justified choice: which encoder layers to adapt. A recently proposed strategy is to probe the encoder, find where phonetic information becomes most decodable, and put the adapters there. We test that inference across two ASR models (Qwen3-ASR, Whisper-small) and two dysarthric corpora (Mandarin CDSD, English UASpeech), holding recipe and seed discipline constant.
  The inference fails. Measured under one pipeline rather than inherited from published figures, all four phoneme-error curves minimise between 83% and 92% of relative depth, each model's minimising layer moving no further between corpora than between probe seeds. The best adaptation band is not similarly constant: none of three natural mappings from the curve to a band -- steepest improvement, lowest band-mean error, or knee location -- recovers the best tested configuration. We do not claim no statistic of the curve could work, only that these three do not.
  Placement still matters, but is model- and corpus-specific. On CDSD the models disagree -- Qwen3-ASR prefers its upper third (29.88% CER, three-seed mean), Whisper-small all-block LoRA (53.56%) -- while on UASpeech neither favours the upper band. At three seeds, upper-versus-all is resolved (-1.31 pp, CI [-2.16, -0.25]) but upper-versus-middle is not (-0.83 pp, CI [-2.06, +1.86]). The two models' preferences differ as a directly tested interaction (-4.71 pp, CI [-6.17, -2.89]); we designate this the primary claim, and record that the designation was made post hoc. On Qwen3-ASR x UASpeech, adapting near the input is reliably worse than not adapting at all (+9.90 pp CER). We recommend choosing the band by a short adaptation sweep on the model and corpus in hand, not by reading it off a probe.

</details>

Abstract: We introduce reinforcement learning from collective rewards (RLCR), a recipe for jointly training language models to act as agent organisations under latency constraints. A CEO and up to eight specialists share one policy, co-ordinate through a message board and workspace, and are trained with a common outcome-derived advantage. The deadline makes parallel work valuable when it increases the return earned before the cap. We train Avalon-M1-Mini (120B) across 147 environments. For the trained model, eight-hire organisational execution raises IMO-ProofBench-Advanced scores from 23.8% to 39.0% relative to single-agent execution. Across 12 benchmarks, organisations achieve similar average scores at approximately one-third the derived latency of token-matched single-agent execution and just over half that of eight independent attempts followed by selection. The trained model also outperforms Nemotron 3 Super, post-trained from the same base model, across all evaluations when comparing each model's best tested configuration. On EdgeBench, organisations overtake the single agent after approximately 0.4 derived hours. Over the course of training, organisations develop hierarchies to manage communication costs, invest more in peer review, and alter how work is divided. Safety evaluations find near-universal refusal of harmful actions but sustained declines in external reporting in two of three oversight scenarios. These results establish a shared-advantage baseline for studying organisational learning, performance-latency trade-offs, and collective oversight.

</details>

Abstract: Changes in image viewpoint and question formulation can alter latent knowledge representations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, output correctness and overall representational drift provide limited insight into how these changes affect model decisions. We therefore evaluate latent knowledge stability across visual and linguistic elicitation conditions from two complementary perspectives: answer preference and internal support. Specifically, we devise the margin retention score (MRS), which measures the first-order projected degradation of the initial answer's logit margin over competing options across conditions and assesses how changes in latent knowledge representations affect this margin. We also present latent knowledge support-structure stability (KSS), which evaluates reference-correct items by comparing internal support profiles constructed using the margin gradient under the reference condition, which jointly reflects changes in how internal support is distributed and changes in total support magnitude. We further construct MMStableBench, comprising 19,452 knowledge items (facts), each probing the same latent knowledge across varied image viewpoints, semantically equivalent question formulations, and answer-option permutations. Experiments across 15 MLLMs show that MRS and KSS reveal latent knowledge instability not captured by accuracy, while an MRS-inspired regularizer further improves the generalization of edited latent knowledge.

</details>

Abstract: Deep search is a critical capability for agents tackling complex, open-ended tasks, where solving a problem requires iterative information gathering, query reformulation, and evidence-based verification rather than a single retrieval step. To address these requirements, we present Bathys 9B and 27B deep search agents built around a training framework that spans verifiable question synthesis, post-training, and online reinforcement learning (RL) under context compression. Our high-quality data construction ensures both search depth and reliability: each clue is reformulated as an indirect reference requiring independent retrieval, and retained evidence chains enable verification that the clues jointly determine a unique answer. Curated trajectories underpin a pipeline of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Kahneman-Tversky Optimization (KTO), and Compaction-Managed Group Sequence Policy Optimization (CM-GSPO): SFT provides a stable foundation for tool use over long horizons and query reformulation; KTO uses binary feedback on trajectories to improve the selection of answers grounded in evidence; CM-GSPO optimizes execution sub-trajectories with length-normalized likelihood ratios on the recorded rollout contexts and reweights sub-trajectory advantages to correct the extra weighting introduced by independently averaging sub-trajectory losses. Bathys 9B and 27B attain 66.51% and 80.49% on BrowseComp, respectively, under an evaluation protocol with trajectory self-verification and conditional retries.

</details>

Abstract: Reasoning models become long-horizon agents through reinforcement learning or, more cheaply, through fine-tuning on a stronger teacher's trajectories, which contain the teacher's chain-of-thought as well as its actions. The standard fine-tuning approach imitates both, yet the strongest teachers are closed models whose chain-of-thought is hidden behind an API. We show that the chain-of-thought can be left out altogether and that the long-horizon policy can be distilled from the teacher's actions alone. The student is trained on these trajectories with its reasoning block left empty, while a KL loss on a replay buffer of its own generations limits drift from its initial model. Surprisingly, the policy learned from these trajectories with an empty reasoning block carries over to inference with thinking enabled, where the student reasons in its own style while acting like the teacher, an effect we call cross-format transfer. Distilling the actions of Qwen3.6-27B raises the task success of Qwen3-4B on AppWorld from 7% to 18% and that of Qwen3-32B from 12% to 25%. We further introduce CoT-proxies, short summaries of the teacher's reasoning placed after the empty reasoning block, which raise Qwen3-4B further, to 35%. Since neither actions nor CoT-proxies need the hidden chain-of-thought, the recipe applies unchanged to closed teachers. Distilling GPT-5.6 Luna from its visible outputs and the reasoning summaries its API returns raises Qwen3-4B from 7% to 48% on AppWorld and from 3% to 28% on SWE-bench Verified.

</details>

Abstract: The computational burden of Model Predictive Control represents a bottleneck limiting its widespread deployment in real-time applications. The selection of the optimal control transcription strategy for a given MPC formulation is a key driver of computational efficiency. Identifying performant MPC transcriptions requires significant effort and domain knowledge. We propose an automated pipeline for the design and evaluation of performant optimal control transcriptions for a given MPC formulation. Custom multi-objective design metrics, such as computation time or closed-loop performance, can be specified and are minimized by the pipeline. As a transcription method, a pseudospectral Legendre-Gauss-Radau method is employed. We show that it incorporates forward Euler integration and implicit collocation methods of low and high order in one formulation, leveraging the pipeline to evaluate both numerical approaches. Furthermore, different mesh configurations can be parameterized. To identify the optimal configuration of the transcription, a Bayesian Optimization framework is applied. The pipelines improvements in MPC performance are demonstrated through On-Orbit Servicing scenarios involving spacecraft motion planning and closed-loop control.

</details>

Abstract: Level 4 autonomous vehicles require fault-tolerant architectures that preserve critical driving functions under sensing, computing, communication, power, and actuation failures. As these systems progress toward industrial scalability, architectural complexity, validation burden, integration effort, and lifecycle cost become increasingly relevant. This study proposes an Operational Design Domain (ODD)-based, safety-constrained systems-engineering framework for architecture rationalization. ODD-specific capabilities, physical fault assumptions, post-fault operational policies, and spatial sensing requirements are propagated through an integrated safety-feasibility gate. Architecture transformations, including removal, relocation, functional reassignment, and consolidation, are evaluated only within the resulting safety-feasible design space, followed by lifecycle-cost sensitivity and robust multiobjective Pareto analysis. A generic, synthetically parameterized Level 4 architecture is used as a demonstrative computational case study. Within this modeled design space, direct ablation showed limited rationalization potential, whereas directional redistribution and functional consolidation generated a broader set of safety-feasible candidates. Under the illustrative lifecycle-cost model, the best nominal candidate achieved a 5.55% normalized cost reduction and remained cost-reducing across all 5,000 sampled points in the prescribed uncertainty domain. The analysis identified 15 non-dominated architecture-policy solutions representing seven physical transformations. The results support capability-based, policy-conditioned, and lifecycle-aware architecture rationalization under the stated assumptions, with safety retained as a non-compensatory feasibility constraint. The reported transformations are design candidates rather than validated production architectures.

</details>
